Pluripotential Operadic Calculus Thanks: Financial support from AGAUR, Govern de Catalunya, through the FI Program and from the Spanish State Research Agency through projects PID2020-117971GB-C22, PID2024-155646NB-I00 and EUR2023-143450.
Anna Sopena-Gilboy

Abstract
. This paper explores a new context for operadic calculus and Koszul duality arising in the study of complex manifolds or, more abstractly, of bidifferential bigraded algebras up to pluripotential weak equivalence. In particular, we establish a Pluripotential Homotopy Transfer Theorem. This provides a homotopical framework for the theory of Massey products in Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies, defined for any complex manifold.



1.
Introduction

The complexified de Rham algebra of differential forms of a complex manifold
admits a decomposition into
(p,q)
-differential forms. This decomposition splits the exterior differential into two components
d=\partial+{\bar{\partial}}
, making it into a bicomplex. From such an object, one can compute several cohomological invariants aside from the cohomology with respect to the total differential: most notably Dolbeault cohomology
[
13
]
, as well as Aeppli
[
1
]
and Bott–Chern cohomology
[
5
]
, given respectively by:

H_{\bar{\partial}}:=\frac{\ker{\bar{\partial}}}{\mathrm{Im}\,{\bar{\partial}}},\quad H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}:=\frac{\ker\partial\cap\ker{\bar{\partial}}}{\mathrm{Im}\,\partial{\bar{\partial}}},\quad H_{\mathcal{A}}:=\frac{\ker\partial{\bar{\partial}}}{\mathrm{Im}\,\partial+\mathrm{Im}\,{\bar{\partial}}}.

These cohomologies have been intensively studied in complex geometry as algebraic invariants carrying
complex-analytic information. In particular, Bott-Chern cohomology plays a key role in the study of non-Kähler geometry, deformation theory, and special Hermitian metrics (see for instance
[
3
]
).

If we additionally consider the multiplicative structure of the differential forms one obtains a commutative bidifferential bigraded algebra (cbba for short).
Motivated by the behavior of wedge products with respect to harmonic forms of complex manifolds,
Angella and Tomassini
[
2
]
introduced
triple Massey-like products defined for Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies, called
ABC-Massey products
. In
[
24
]
, Milivojevic and Stelzig extend these products to higher arities and explore notions of formality obstructed by these invariants.
This leads to the consideration of
pluripotential weak equivalences
, defined by those maps inducing
isomorphisms in both Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies.
Remarkably, compact Kähler manifolds can have non-trivial ABC-Massey products, showing that the pluripotential weak equivalence class of the complexified de Rham algebra is a finer invariant than its cohomology with the real Hodge structure
[
26
]
.

In
[
38
]
, Stelzig studies the homotopy theory of cbba’s over a field of characteristic zero with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences, through the construction of minimal models of cbba’s à la Sullivan. With this theory, Stelzig obtains various refinements of
the homotopy groups of complex manifolds, sensitive to the analytic structure.

A complementary method to study homotopy types of differential graded algebras is through the so-called Homotopy Transfer Theory: over a field, one can always construct a contraction connecting a dga to its cohomology, which induces an
A_{\infty}
-structure on cohomology, unique up to
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism
[
17
]
. This
A_{\infty}
-structure is closely related to Massey products and encodes the homotopy type of the dga. When applied to the differential forms of a smooth manifold or, more generally, to the piece-wise linear forms of a topological space, the
A_{\infty}
-structure is in fact a
C_{\infty}
-structure and encodes the rational homotopy type of the space.

In this paper, we develop homotopy transfer theory in the pluripotential setting.
One main advantage of our approach with respect to Stelzig’s theory is that our setting works without the connectivity assumptions. Moreover,
it situates ABC-Massey products in a robust homotopical framework in which new higher operations emerge naturally.

To formalize our theory, we establish a new operadic framework: we work with operads in the symmetric monoidal category of bicomplexes over a field of characteristic zero, and construct minimal models of operads with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences in two complementary ways. First, following the more classical approach of Markl, Shnider, and Stasheff
[
21
]
we obtain minimal models for any reduced operad using free extensions. In our setting, the notion of minimal operad requires cofibrancy
together with the condition that the composition
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
is decomposable. We prove:

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad in bicomplexes such that
\mathcal{P}(0)=0
and
\mathcal{P}(1)=\mathbf{k}
. Then, there exists a minimal operad
\mathcal{M}
and a pluripotential weak equivalence
\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}
.

The second way is through Koszul duality. We use the
inflation functor
, defined in
[
20
]
, from cochain complexes to bicomplexes

\mathrm{Inf}:\mathrm{Ch}\to\mathrm{BiCo}

as part of a Quillen adjunction, with right adjoint given by
a well-known sheaf-theoretic construction in complex geometry
[
12
]
,
[
31
]
,
[
25
]
.
This functor allows us to recycle many of the computations of the dg case. For an operad
\mathcal{P}
concentraded in bidegreee
(0,0)
with trivial differentials we define
\mathcal{P}_{\infty}^{pp}
as the free operad generated by
\mathrm{Inf}(s\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\mbox{!`}})
with two additional differentials. Here,
s\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\mbox{!`}}
denotes the suspension of the Koszul dual cooperad of
\mathcal{P}
viewed as a dg-operad. We show:

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Koszul operad in bicomplexes. The operad

\mathcal{P}_{\infty}^{pp}
is the pluripotential minimal model of
\mathcal{P}
.

Algebras over the minimal model of the associative operad are called
pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebras
(
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras for short). As expected, these are algebraic structures in which associativity holds up to higher homotopies in the pluripotential sense: there is a family of
2n-3
operations of arity
n\geq 2
and, in particular,
there is a single binary operation
\mu_{2}
of bidegree zero satisfying

\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})-\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu_{2})=[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{3}]].

Here,
\mu_{3}
is a ternary operation of bidegree
(-1,-1)
. Note that this equation states that
\mu_{3}
is a homotopy measuring the failure of
\mu_{2}
to be associative. Indeed,
a pluripotential homotopy between morphisms of bicomplexes
f
and
g
is given by a map
h
satisfying

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]]=f-g,

where
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
denote the two differentials of the bicomplexes.

In the classical setting, homotopy transfer is carried into a minimal chain complex, characterized by having trivial differential.
Here, minimality is achieved
by bicomplexes such that the composition of the two differentials is trivial:
\partial{\bar{\partial}}=0
.
We prove:

Theorem C
.

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
be a bidifferential bigraded algebra.

(1)

There is a contraction into a minimal bicomplex

\hbox to172.06pt{\vbox to37.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 107.35123pt\lower-18.52716pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(2)

There is a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure on
(H,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
and a pluripotential
\infty
-weak equivalence
g:H\rightsquigarrow A
.

Note in the classical setting, minimal complexes are
isomorphic to the cohomology. In the pluripotential case,
H
is a bicomplex with possibly non-trivial differentials, and the minimality ensures that this is isomorphic to

H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(H)\oplus\tilde{H}_{\mathcal{A}}(H)
, where
\tilde{H}_{\mathcal{A}}(H)
is the cokernel of the map
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(H)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(H)
from Bott-Chern to Aeppli cohomology. Morally, this result states that, under a choice of sections, any bicomplex admits its ’pluripotential cohomology’ as a deformation retract, and one can transfer the multiplicative structure along this retract.

In arity three, Theorem
C
recovers the
ABC-Massey products of
[
2
]
. In the case of complex manifolds, this gives new biholomorphic invariants. In fact, we prove a commutative version of the above theorem (Theorem
2.34
) which gives
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra structures encoding the pluripotential homotopy type of the complex manifold. More generally, we prove:

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a Koszul operad. There is an equivalence of homotopy categories

\mathrm{Ho}(\mathcal{P}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg})\cong\mathrm{Ho}(\infty\text{\,-}\mathcal{P}^{pp}_{\infty}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}).

Here,
\mathcal{P}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
denotes the category of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras while

\infty\text{\,-}\mathcal{P}^{pp}_{\infty}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
denotes the category of
\mathcal{P}^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras whose morphisms are
\infty
-morphisms, arising from pluripotential Koszul duality theory. The localization
\mathrm{Ho}
is with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences.

When applied to the complexified de Rham algebra of differential forms of a complex manifold, the results of this paper establish a robust homotopical setting that translates analytical information of complex structures into algebraic data. This offers a new framework to study the so-called strong and weak formality and, more generally, pluripotential homotopy types of complex manifolds. This is an active area of research, central in the recent works mentioned at the beginning of this introduction, as well as in
[
32
]
,
[
27
]
and
[
30
]
, to name a few.

We briefly explain the structure of this paper.
To make the paper accessible to geometers, we begin the first section by giving explicit definitions for the case of
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-
algebras and sketch the connections to geometry, avoiding the harder operadic machinery. In the subsequent sections we justify that these definitions are the natural ones from a homotopical point of view and develop the surrounding theory. Namely, the paper is organized as follows: in Section
2
we define
pluripotential
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras and prove the Homotopy Transfer Theorem in the associative and commutative cases (Theorem
2.29
and Theorem
2.34
). We also give an explicit relation between transferred structures and triple ABC-Massey products. In Section
3
we review some constructions for operads and cooperads in the category of bicomplexes. In Section
4
we show existence and uniqueness of minimal models of operads in bicomplexes with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences (Theorem
4.12
). In Section
5
we develop Koszul duality for operads in bicomplexes in bidegree
(0,0)
with trivial differentials and prove Theorem
5.22
on minimal models. Lastly, in Section

6
we define the bar and cobar constructions for operads in bicomplexes with respect to a twisting morphism and prove Theorem
6.14
on the equivalence of homotopy categories.

Notation and conventions

Througout this work,
\mathbf{k}
denotes a field of characteristic zero. For a pure
(bi)degree element
v\in V
of a bigraded object we use the notation
|v|
for the
total degree.

Acknowledgments

I would like to thank my advisors Joana Cirici and Jonas Stelzig for their guidance and support. I am grateful to Muriel Livernet and Bruno Vallette for their dedication and discussions when I visited them in Paris. Thanks also to Ricardo Campos and Geoffroy Horel for useful comments.

2.
Pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebras and homotopy transfer theorem

In this section we introduce the category of pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebras and prove the Pluripotential Homotopy Transfer Theorem for bidifferential bigraded algebras, giving explicit formulas. At the end of the section, we relate Pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-structures with ABC-Massey products defined for complex manifolds
[
2
]
.

2.1.
Bicomplexes and pluripotential weak equivalences

We review the main properties of the category

\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
of bicomplexes over
\mathbf{k}
(also called double complexes in the literature). All results claimed without proof in this section, can be found in
[
38
]
. Objects in this category are given by bigraded vector spaces

A=\bigoplus_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}A^{p,q}

together with two linear maps
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}

of bidegrees
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively, and
satisfying the relations

\partial^{2}=0,\quad{\bar{\partial}}^{2}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial{\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}\partial=0.

Morphisms in this category are given by bidegree-preserving linear maps compatible with both
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Although we use the notation
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
for the differentials of a bicomplex, borrowed from the notation of complex manifolds, our bicomplexes are defined over an arbitrary field, so we do not assume any real structure.

The category
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
is a symmetric monoidal category, with the tensor product given by

(A\otimes B)^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{r,s\in\mathbb{Z}}A^{r,s}\otimes B^{p-r,q-s}

with differentials given by the Leibniz rule. The unit is given by the field
\mathbf{k}
concentrated in bidegree
(0,0)
.

The internal Hom is defined by

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(A,B)^{p,q}=\prod_{r,s\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}(A^{r,s},B^{r+p,s+q}),

with differentials

\partial f:=[\partial,f]\quad\text{ and }\quad{\bar{\partial}}f:=[{\bar{\partial}},f],

where
[-,-]
is the graded commutator

[f,g]:=f\circ g-(-1)^{|f|\cdot|g|}g\circ f.

The internal Hom and tensor product form an adjunction

(-\otimes A):\mathrm{\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}}\;\rightleftarrows\;\mathrm{\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}}:\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(A,-).

The category
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
is then a closed symmetric monoidal category.

Associated to a bicomplex
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
there are various functorial cohomologies:
aside from the
\partial
- and
{\bar{\partial}}
-cohomologies

H^{*,*}_{\partial}(A):={\frac{\mathrm{Ker}(\partial)}{\mathrm{Im}(\partial)}}\quad\text{ and }\quad H^{*,*}_{\bar{\partial}}(A):=\frac{\mathrm{Ker}({\bar{\partial}})}{\mathrm{Im}({\bar{\partial}})},

we also have the Bott-Chern
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}
and Aeppli
H_{\mathcal{A}}
cohomologies, given respectively by

H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{*,*}(A):={\frac{\mathrm{Ker}({\bar{\partial}})\cap\mathrm{Ker}(\partial)}{\mathrm{Im}({\bar{\partial}}\partial)}}\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{\mathcal{A}}^{*,*}(A):={\frac{\mathrm{Ker}({\bar{\partial}}\partial)}{\mathrm{Im}({\bar{\partial}})+\mathrm{Im}(\partial)}}.

Note that the identity induces natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\partial}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{dR}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{{\bar{\partial}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}}}

where
H_{dR}
denotes the total cohomology. Moreover, the row and column filtrations on a bicomplex
A
induce two spectral sequences relating the total cohomology with
\partial
- and
{\bar{\partial}}
- cohomologies, respectively:

H_{\partial}(A)\Rightarrow H_{dR}(A)\Leftarrow H_{{\bar{\partial}}}(A).

A bicomplex
A
is said to
satisfy the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property
if the map of bigraded vector spaces

H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)

is injective (and hence this map, and all the maps in the above diagram, are isomorphisms).

Let
\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}
and
\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
be the functors which send a bicomplex
A
to the kernel and cokernel of
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)
, respectively. Explicitly, this gives:

\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\partial\cap\mathrm{Ker}{\bar{\partial}}+\mathrm{Im}{\bar{\partial}}\cap\mathrm{Ker}\partial}{\mathrm{Im}\partial{\bar{\partial}}},\qquad\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{A}}=\frac{\mathrm{Ker}\partial{\bar{\partial}}}{\mathrm{Im}\partial+\mathrm{Im}{\bar{\partial}}+\mathrm{Ker}\partial\cap\mathrm{Ker}{\bar{\partial}}}.

The
ABC-cohomology
of a bicomplex
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
is the bicomplex defined by

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A):=H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\oplus\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{A}}(A)

together with the two differentials
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
induced from those of
A
. Note that this bicomplex is
pluripotentially minimal
, in the sense that
\partial{\bar{\partial}}\equiv 0
.

We will use the following result:

Lemma 2.2
.

Any short exact sequence of bicomplexes

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0

induces a long exact sequence

\displaystyle\dots\to H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}^{p+q-2}(B)\to H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}^{p+q-2}(C)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(B)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(C)\to

\displaystyle\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{p,q}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{p,q}(B)\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{p,q}(C)\to H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}^{p+q+1}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}^{p+q+1}(B)\to\dots

where
H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}^{k}
denotes the cohomology of the Bigolin complex in degree
k
.

Any bicomplex
A
admits a (noncanonical) decomposition

A=A_{\mathrm{zig}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{sq}}

where
A_{\mathrm{sq}}
is a direct sum of squares, of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{k},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\partial}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\partial}}}

and
A_{\mathrm{zig}}
is a bicomplex satisfying
\partial{\bar{\partial}}=0
. Therefore,
A_{\mathrm{zig}}
is a direct sum of bicomplexes of the form

\hbox to24.68pt{\vbox to14.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.34024pt\lower-7.13194pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

In squares and zig-zags,
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
are isomorphisms. This result has long been known as a folklore statement. Complete proofs can be found in
[
37
]
and
[
18
]
.

With such a decompostion, we have
A_{\mathrm{zig}}\cong H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
.
In particular, the summand
A_{\mathrm{sq}}
is pluripotentially acyclic. If the bicomplex satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-lemma property, the bicomplex
A_{\mathrm{zig}}
has trivial differentials, and hence the bicomplex
A
splits as a direct sum of squares and a bigraded vector space.

The category of bicomplexes admits a model structure whose weak equivalences are defined in terms of Bott–Chern and Aeppli cohomologies. The rest of this section reviews this model structure and the relevant definitions.

Definition 2.3
.

A morphism of bicomplexes
f\colon A\to B
is said to be a
pluripotential weak equivalence

if
H_{\mathcal{A}}(f)
and
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(f)
are isomorphisms.

Remark 2.4
.

Being a pluripotential weak equivalence
f\colon A\to B
is equivalent to inducing an isomorphism

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(f)

at the level of ABC-cohomology. In
[
38
]
, pluripotential weak equivalences are also called
pluripotential quasi-isomorphisms
or
bigraded quasi-isomorphisms
.

Remark 2.5
.

A bicomplex
A
is called
bounded
if
A^{p,q}=0
for all but finitely many
(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
f:A\to B
is a map between bounded bicomplexes, then
f
is a pluripotential weak equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism in cohomology with respect to
\partial
and with respect to
{\bar{\partial}}
.

The category
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
admits a cofibrantly generated model structure,
where:

•

fibrations are surjective morphisms,

•

weak equivalences are pluripotential weak equivalences,

•

cofibrations are injective morphisms.

We will refer to this as the
pluripotential model structure
. It is easy to see that every object is fibrant and cofibrant. The tensor product and internal hom make
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
into a symmetric monoidal model
category. The notion of homotopy associated with this model structure is the following.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
f,g:A\to B
be two maps of bicomplexes. A
homotopy from
f
to
g
is a linear map
h:A\to B
of bidegree
(-1,-1)

satisfying

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]]=f-g.

If such a homotopy exists, we write
f\simeq g
.

Since we are working over a field, the class of homotopy equivalences induced from the above definition coincides with the class of pluripotential weak equivalences.

Proposition 2.7
.

Any two homotopic maps
f,g:A\to B
of bicomplexes induce the same map in cohomology:

[f]_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}=[g]_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}:H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)\quad\text{ and }\quad[f]_{\mathcal{A}}=[g]_{\mathcal{A}}:H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(B).

We will use the following notion of shift functor
\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
.

The following definition introduces the pluripotential analogue of the mapping cone for cochain complexes. It will be used to detect pluripotential weak equivalences.

Definition 2.8
.

The
mapping cone
of a morphism of bicomplexes
f\colon A\to B
is the bicomplex defined by

C(f):=\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(A)\oplus B,

with differentials given by

\displaystyle\partial(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)=(\partial a,-\partial a^{\prime},-\partial a^{\prime\prime}-a,\partial b+f(a^{\prime})),

\displaystyle{\bar{\partial}}(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)=({\bar{\partial}}a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime}+a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},{\bar{\partial}}b+f(a^{\prime\prime})).

There is a short exact sequence

B\to C(f)\to\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}A

and

f
is a pluripotential weak equivalence if and only if
C(f)
is acyclic:
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(f))=0
.

We next state Künneth formulae for Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies. There are natural maps

H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\otimes H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)\longrightarrow H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes B)

and

H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\otimes H_{\mathcal{A}}(B)\;\oplus\;H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)\otimes H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)\longrightarrow H_{\mathcal{A}}(A\otimes B).

These maps are neither injective nor surjective in general.
Below, we provide Künneth-type formulas for
H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}
cohomology, although the resulting isomorphisms are not natural.
For canonical descriptions of the kernels and cokernels of the maps above, see Theorem 1.35 in
[
38
]
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
A
and
B
be two bicomplexes. Then, there exists an isomorphism

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes B)\cong H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\otimes H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)).

Proof.

If
A\simeq B
is a pluripotential homotopy equivalence, then

A\otimes C\simeq B\otimes C
as well. One may take a homotopy of the form

h\otimes\mathrm{id}_{C}
, where
h
is a homotopy witnessing
A\simeq B
. Therefore, by Theorem 1.21 in
[
38
]
, for any choice of decomposition into squares and zig-zags, we obtain

A\otimes B\simeq A_{{\mathrm{zig}}}\otimes B_{\mathrm{zig}}.

Choosing an isomorphism
H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\cong A_{{\mathrm{zig}}}
and
H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)\cong B_{\mathrm{zig}}
and using Proposition
2.7
we obtain an isomorphism

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A\otimes B)\cong H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\otimes H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(B)).\qed

Remark 2.10
.

The proposition above relies on the fact that any bicomplex
A
splits (non-uniquely) as a
direct sum of squares and zig-zags,

A=A_{{\mathrm{zig}}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{sq}}.

The hypotheses required for such a decomposition can be weakened to bicomplexes in other
abelian categories, for instance, to bicomplexes of characteristic-zero representations of a
finite group
G
, see Remark 5 in
[
37
]
.
Consequently, Proposition
2.9
also applies to bicomplexes in

\mathbf{k}[\mathbb{S}_{n}]
-modules, a fact we will use later.

2.2.
The category of
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras

We now introduce the category of pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebras. As we will see in Section
5
, these arise as algebras over the pluripotential minimal model of the associative operad.

Originally,
A_{\infty}
-algebras were introduced by Stasheff in
[
36
]
in his study of loop spaces and their higher homotopy associativity. We briefly recall the classical definition:
an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
consists of a cochain complex
(A,d)
together with a family of linear maps

\mu_{n}:A^{\otimes n}\to A

of degree
2-n
for every
n\geq 2
, satisfying the following relation:

[d,\mu_{n}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n+1\\
1\leq r\leq k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{r(1+\ell)+n}\mu_{k}\left(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mu_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes s}\right)

where

[d,\mu_{n}]:=d\mu_{n}-\sum_{i}(-1)^{n}\mu_{n}(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes d\otimes\mathrm{id}^{n-i}).

For low arities we have the following relations:

•

For
n=2
we have

d\mu_{2}=\mu_{2}(d\otimes\mathrm{id})+\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes d)

•

For
n=3
we have

[d,\mu_{3}]=\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})-\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu_{2}).

An
A_{\infty}
-algebra can be viewed as a chain complex equipped with a binary operation that is associative up to a coherent system of higher homotopies. In this sense, a pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebra is analogous, with ordinary homotopy replaced by pluripotential homotopy. Maybe surprisingly, these two definitions are quite different as one can see below.

Definition 2.11
.

A
pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebra
,
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra for short, consists of a bicomplex
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
together
n
-ary operations of bidegree
(p,q)

\mu_{n}^{p,q}:A^{\otimes n}\to A

for every
n\geq 2
, where the operations are nonzero only in the following cases:

•

when
p+q=2-n
and
p,q\leq 0
,

•

when
p+q=1-n
and
p,q\leq-1
.

These operations must satisfy the following relations:

(
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}_{n}
)

[\partial,\mu_{n}^{p,q}]=q\mu_{n}^{p+1,q}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n+1\\
1\leq i\leq k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p+1\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{i(\ell+1)+n+\varepsilon}\mu_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mu_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes k-i}),

and

(
\mathcal{\overline{A}}^{p,q}_{n}
)

[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{n}^{p,q}]=-p\mu_{n}^{p,q+1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n+1\\
1\leq i\leq k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{i(\ell+1)+n+\varepsilon}\mu_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\mu_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes k-i}),

where
\varepsilon=(p_{1}+q_{1})(\ell+p_{2}+q_{2})+(p+q+n).

Remark 2.12
.

For a fixed
n
, there are
2n-3
operations:
n-1
of them have total degree
2-n
, and
n-2
of them have total degree
1-n
. The relations can be represented as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\partial,\mu_{n}^{p,q}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=\,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\mu_{n}^{p+1,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum}\pm}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}}

n
n

Here, the sum runs over all compositions of arity
n
(as in
A_{\infty}
-algebras) and bidegree
(p+1,q)
. Notice that if
p+q=2-n
, the first tree in the picture vanishes, and all operations in the summation satisfy
p_{i}+q_{i}=2-n
by degree reasons. On the other hand, if
p+q=1-n
, then in each composition in the summation, exactly one operation satisfies
p_{i}+q_{i}=1-n
.

Remark 2.13
.

Note that the tuples
(A,\partial,\{\mu_{\bullet}^{*,0}\})
and
(A,{\bar{\partial}},\{\mu_{\bullet}^{0,*}\})
, arising from a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\{\mu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
, are
A_{\infty}
-algebras.

Example 2.14
.

A
bidifferential bigraded algebra
is a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra with
\mu_{n}^{p,q}=0
for all
n\geq 3
.

Example 2.15
.

Let us break down the previous definition in the case of low arities.

•

For
n=2
, there is a unique bidegree-preserving binary operation

\mu_{2}:A^{\otimes 2}\to A

satisfying

\partial\mu_{2}=\mu_{2}(\partial,\mathrm{id})+\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id},\partial)\quad\text{ and }\quad{\bar{\partial}}\mu_{2}=\mu_{2}({\bar{\partial}},\mathrm{id})+\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id},{\bar{\partial}}).

This is equivalent to say that
both
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
are derivations with respect to
\mu_{2}
.

•

For
n=3
, we have three ternary operations

\mu_{3}^{p,q}:A^{\otimes 3}\longrightarrow A,\qquad(p,q)\in\{(-1,0),\,(0,-1),\,(-1,-1)\},

satisfying

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial,\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,0}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})-\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu_{2}),

\displaystyle\quad\bigl[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,0}\bigr]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial,\mu_{3}^{0,\text{-}1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\quad\bigl[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{3}^{0,\text{-}1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})-\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu_{2}),

\displaystyle\bigl[\partial,\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=-\,\mu_{3}^{0,\text{-}1},

\displaystyle\quad\bigl[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,0}.

In particular, these imply

\bigl[\partial,\,\bigl[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}\bigr]\bigr]\;=\;\mu_{2}(\mu_{2}\otimes\mathrm{id})-\mu_{2}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mu_{2}).

Thus,
\mu_{2}
is associative up to a pluripotential homotopy given by
\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}
. Note that
\mu^{\text{-}1,0}_{3}
and
\mu^{0,\text{-}1}_{3}
are determined by
\mu^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}_{3}
, in fact this holds in general, see the next remark for a detailed explanation.

Remark 2.16
.

Note that

[\partial,\mu_{n}^{p-1,q}]-[{\bar{\partial}},\mu_{n}^{p,q-1}]=(p+q)\mu_{n}^{p,q},

for any
n\geq 2
and
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. This means that, in arity
n
, the
n-2
operations of total degree
1-n
determine the
n-1
operations of total degree
2-n
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
(A,\{\mu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
and
(B,\{\nu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
be two
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras. A

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism

f:A\rightsquigarrow B
is a family of linear maps

f_{n}^{p,q}:A^{\otimes n}\to B,\quad n\geq 2,

of bidegree
(p,q)

that are non-zero only in
the following cases:

•

when
p+q=1-n
and
p,q\leq 0
,

•

when
p+q=-n
and
p,q\leq-1

such that

\displaystyle[\partial,f_{n}^{p,q}]=

\displaystyle qf_{n}^{p+1,q}-\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{0}+p_{i_{1}}+\dots+p_{i_{k}}=p+1\\
q_{0}+q_{i_{1}}+\dots+q_{i_{k}}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\theta(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})+\varepsilon_{1}}\,\nu^{p_{0},q_{0}}_{k}\Bigl(f_{i_{1}}^{p_{i_{1}},q_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{k}}^{p_{i_{k}},q_{i_{k}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n+1\\
r+s+1=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p+1\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{r(1+\ell)+k+\varepsilon_{2}}\,f_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\Bigl(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mu^{p_{2},q_{2}}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes s}\Bigr),

and

\displaystyle[{\bar{\partial}},f_{n}^{p,q}]=

\displaystyle-pf_{n}^{p,q+1}-\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{0}+p_{i_{1}}+\dots+p_{i_{k}}=p\\
q_{0}+q_{i_{1}}+\dots+q_{i_{k}}=q+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\theta(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})+\varepsilon_{1}}\,\nu^{p_{0},q_{0}}_{k}\Bigl(f_{i_{1}}^{p_{i_{1}},q_{i_{1}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{k}}^{p_{i_{k}},q_{i_{k}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n+1\\
r+s+1=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q+1\end{subarray}}(-1)^{r(1+\ell)+k+\varepsilon_{2}}\,f_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\Bigl(\mathrm{id}^{\otimes r}\otimes\mu^{p_{2},q_{2}}_{\ell}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{\otimes s}\Bigr).

Here,

\theta:=\theta(i_{1},\dots,i_{k}):=\sum_{1\leq r<s\leq k}i_{r}(i_{s}+1),

\varepsilon_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}(p_{r}+q_{r})\right)(p_{j}+q_{j}+i_{j}+1)+p+q+n+1,

and

\varepsilon_{2}=(p_{1}+q_{1})(p_{2}+q_{2}+\ell)+p+q+n+1.

Remark 2.18
.

For every
n\geq 1
there are
2n-1
linear maps,
n
of total degree
1-n
and
n-1
of total degree
-n
.

Example 2.19
.

Again, let us break down the definition of

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphisms in the case of low arities.

•

For
n=1
, we have a single degree-preserving map

f_{1}:A\to B,

such that

[\partial,f_{1}]=0\quad\text{and}\quad[{\bar{\partial}},f_{1}]=0.

In other words,
f_{1}
is a map of bicomplexes.

•

For
n=2
, we have three linear maps

f_{2}^{p,q}:A^{\otimes 2}\to B

of bidegrees
(-1,0)
,
(0,-1)
, and
(-1,-1)
satisfying

\displaystyle[\partial,f_{2}^{\text{-}1,0}]

\displaystyle=-\mu_{2}^{B}\bigl(f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\bigr)+f_{1}\bigl(\mu_{2}^{A}\bigr),

\displaystyle[\partial,f_{2}^{0,\text{-}1}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\partial,f_{2}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}]

\displaystyle=-f_{2}^{0,\text{-}1},

and similarly,

\displaystyle[{\bar{\partial}},f_{2}^{\text{-}1,0}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[{\bar{\partial}},f_{2}^{0,\text{-}1}]

\displaystyle=-\mu_{2}^{B}\bigl(f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\bigr)+f_{1}\bigl(\mu_{2}^{A}\bigr),

\displaystyle[{\bar{\partial}},f_{2}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}]

\displaystyle=f_{2}^{\text{-}1,0}.

In particular, we have

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},f_{2}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}]]=-\mu_{2}^{B}\bigl(f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\bigr)+f_{1}\bigl(\mu_{2}^{A}\bigr).

Remark 2.20
.

Note again that

[\partial,f_{n}^{p-1,q}]+[{\bar{\partial}},f_{n}^{p,q-1}]=(p+q)f_{n}^{p,q},

so, in arity
n
, the
n-1
linear maps of total degree
-n
determine the
n
linear maps of total degree
1-n
.

Definition 2.21
.

A

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism
f:A\rightsquigarrow B
is called a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence
if
f_{1}
is a pluripotential weak equivalence. We say that
f
is
strict
if
f_{n}=0\text{ for all }n\geq 2.
The
identity

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism
is the strict morphism with
f_{1}=\mathrm{id}_{A}
.

The composition of two
\infty
-morphisms
g:A\rightsquigarrow B
and
f:B\rightsquigarrow C
, is given by

(f\circ g)_{n}=\sum_{i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{0}+\dots+p_{k}=p\\
q_{0}+\dots+q_{k}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\theta(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})+\varepsilon}\,f_{k}^{p_{0},q_{0}}\Bigl(g_{i_{1}}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes g_{i_{k}}^{p_{k},q_{k}}\Bigr),

where the sign
{\theta(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})}
is defined as in the

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism relations and

\varepsilon:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{r=0}^{j-1}(p_{r}+q_{r})\right)(p_{j}+q_{j}+i_{j}+1).

This composition is associative, since

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphisms are precisely morphisms between the images of the source and the target under the bar construction
\mathrm{B}_{\iota}
, see Section
6.1
.

2.3.
Homotopy transfer theorem

In this section, we prove that every bicomplex admits a contraction into a minimal bicomplex. Furthermore, we show that any bba induces a pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on any homotopy equivalent bicomplex, with explicit formulas.

Definition 2.22
.

A
contraction
of a bicomplex

(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
into a bicomplex
(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
f
and
g
are bicomplex morphisms such that

fg=\mathrm{id}_{B}
and

h
is a pluripotential homotopy from the
gf
to the identity:

gf-\mathrm{id}_{A}=[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]].

Proposition 2.23
.

Every bicomplex
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
admits a contraction
into a minimal bicomplex:

\hbox to172.06pt{\vbox to37.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 107.35123pt\lower-18.52716pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-103.62547pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}};\,\text{ with }\partial{\bar{\partial}}=0\text{ on }H.

Proof.

By Section
2.1
, we can decompose (non-uniquely) every bicomplex
A
as

A=A_{\mathrm{sq}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{zig}}.

Let

H:=A_{\mathrm{zig}}\simeq A.

We define
f:A\twoheadrightarrow H
and
g:H\hookrightarrow A
via the decomposition. Next, we define the homotopy

h:A_{\mathrm{sq}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{zig}}\to A_{\mathrm{sq}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{zig}}

as follows. Given a pair
(a,b)\in A_{\mathrm{sq}}\oplus A_{\mathrm{zig}}
,
we let
h(a,b)=(a^{\prime},0)
if
a=-\partial{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime}
and zero otherwise. Note that since we are considering a splitting into squares and zig-zags,
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
are isomorphisms, so
a^{\prime}
is uniquely determined. This homotopy satisfies

gf-\mathrm{id}_{A}=[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]].

First, observe that for any element
(a,b)\in A_{\text{sq}}\oplus A_{\text{zig}}
, we have:

(gf-\mathrm{id}_{A})(a,b)=-(a,0).

Next, note that

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]](A_{\mathrm{zig}})=0,

by definition.
Now, consider the case when
a\in A_{\mathrm{sq}}
, we will compute

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]]a=(\partial{\bar{\partial}}h-\partial h{\bar{\partial}}+{\bar{\partial}}h\partial-h{\bar{\partial}}\partial)a.

Assume
a
is a degree-homogeneous element in a square, as depicted below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\bar{\partial}}\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial{\bar{\partial}}\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\partial}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\partial}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

There are four possible cases for
a
based on its position in the square:

(1)

If
a
is the top right corner of a square, so that
a=\partial{\bar{\partial}}\alpha
, then the only non zero term is

\partial{\bar{\partial}}ha=-\partial{\bar{\partial}}\alpha=-a.

(2)

If
a
is the bottom right corner of a square, so that
a=\partial\alpha
, then the only non zero term is

-\partial h{\bar{\partial}}a=-\partial h{\bar{\partial}}\partial\alpha=-\partial(\alpha)=-a.

(3)

If
a
is the top left corner of a square, so that
a={\bar{\partial}}\alpha
, then

{\bar{\partial}}h\partial a={\bar{\partial}}h\partial{\bar{\partial}}\alpha=-{\bar{\partial}}\alpha=-a

is the only non-zero term.

(4)

If
a
is the bottom left corner of a square, then

-h{\bar{\partial}}\partial a=-\alpha=-a

is the only non-zero term.

In all cases, we obtain
[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]]a=-a
. Therefore, we conclude

[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},h]](a,b)=-(a,0),

which completes the proof.
∎

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\mu)
be a bidifferential bigraded algebra. For the rest of the section, fix a contraction (not necessarily minimal)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{11}}

and define
h_{10}:=[{\bar{\partial}},h_{11}]
and
h_{01}:=-[\partial,h_{11}]
. Note that these maps are chain homotopies with respect to
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
, respectively:

\mathrm{id}_{A}-gf=[\partial,h_{10}]\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{id}_{A}-gf=[{\bar{\partial}},h_{10}].

One can define a binary operation
\nu_{2}:B\otimes B\to B
by the formula

\nu_{2}:=f\mu(g,g).

This operation is only associative up to pluripotential homotopy. Indeed,
the ternary operation
\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}:B^{\otimes 3}\to B
, defined by the formula

\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}:=f\mu(h_{11}\mu(g,g),g)-f\mu(g,h_{11}\mu(g,g)),

satisfies the relation

\nu_{2}(\nu_{2},\mathrm{id})-\nu_{2}(\mathrm{id},\nu_{2})=[\partial,[{\bar{\partial}},\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}]].

Remark 2.24
.

This operation satisfies

{\bar{\partial}}\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}-\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}{\bar{\partial}}=\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,0}
and
\partial\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}-\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1}\partial=-\nu_{3}^{0,\text{-}1}
, where

\nu_{3}^{\text{-}1,0}=f\mu(h_{10}\mu(g,g),g)-f\mu(g,h_{10}\mu(g,g)),

and

\nu_{3}^{0,\text{-}1}=f\mu(h_{01}\mu(g,g),g)-f\mu(g,h_{01}\mu(g,g)).

In general, one can define the following abstract maps associated to a contraction, called
\mathfrak{p}
-kernels
, which will be used to transfer the algebraic structure of a bba through a minimal contraction.

The associated
\mathfrak{p}
-kernels
are the linear maps

\{\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}:A^{\otimes n}\to A\}_{n\geq 2}

of bidegree
(p,q)

where

•

p+q=2-n
and
p,q\leq 0
, or

•

p+q=1-n
and
p,q\leq-1
.

These are defined recursively by

\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k+\ell=n\\
k,\ell\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}-\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k(\ell+1)+\varepsilon}\mu(h_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\mathfrak{m}_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes h_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\mathfrak{m}_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}),

where
\varepsilon=(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1})(d_{1}+k)+(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}+d_{1})(\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2}+1)+(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}+d_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2})(d_{2}+\ell)
and
d_{x}=p_{x}+q_{x}
for
x=1,2
. We formally write
h_{11}\mathfrak{m}_{1}=\mathrm{id}
,
h_{01}\mathfrak{m}_{1}=0
and
h_{10}\mathfrak{m}_{1}=0
.

Remark 2.25
.

These maps can be defined as linear combinations of planar binary trees with
n
leaves, where each internal edge is labeled by
h_{10}
,
h_{01}
or
h_{11}
. Due to degree constraints, only one leaf decorated with
h_{11}
is permitted per tree. Explicitly, let
\text{PBT}_{n}
the set of all planar binary trees with
n
leaves. Then, we have

\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}=\sum_{t\in\text{PBT}_{n}}\pm\mathfrak{m}_{t}^{p,q},

where

•

if
p+q=2-n
,
\mathfrak{m}_{t}^{p,q}
consists of the sum (with signs) of
\frac{(n-2)!}{|p|!|q|!}
copies of
t
such that each copy is a different way of decorating the internal edges with
|p|
homotopies
h_{10}
and
|q|
homotopies
h_{01}
,

•

if
p+q=1-n
,
\mathfrak{m}_{t}^{p,q}
consists of the sum (with signs) of
\frac{(n-2)!}{(|p|-1)!(|q|-1)!}
copies of
t
such that each copy is a different way of decorating the internal edges with
|p|-1
homotopies
h_{10}
,
|q|-1
homotopies
h_{01}
and one homotopy
h_{11}
.

Example 2.26
.

Below, the operation
\mathfrak{m}_{4}^{-2,-1}
is depicted as a sum of planar binary trees with
n
leaves:

+
-
+
+
-
-
+
-
-
+

Here, the edges that are colored in red are labeled by the homotopy
h_{10}
and the edges that are colored in blue are labeled by the homotopy
h_{11}
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\mu)
be a bidifferential bigraded algebra.
Given a contraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{11}}

define, for all
n\geq 2
,

\nu_{n}^{p,q}:=f\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}g^{\otimes n}\quad\text{ and }\quad g_{n}^{p,q}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i-\alpha=p\\
j-\beta=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(\alpha+\beta)(i+j+n)}h_{\alpha\beta}\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{i,j}g^{\otimes n}.

Then,
(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\{\nu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
is a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra, and
g=(g,\{g_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})

is a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence
g:B\rightsquigarrow A
.

Proof.

In the proof of the analogous result for the classical case of
A_{\infty}
-algebras, Markl
[
22
]
states: “A straightforward but awfully technical induction shows that…” However, the corresponding awfully technical induction is not explicitly presented in the paper. As one might expect, the proof of Lemma
2.27
is even worse. But since I’m just a PhD student, I had no choice but to write out the proof, which can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 2.28
.

Note that, by the definition of
\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}
, we can rewrite
\nu_{n}^{p,q}
and
g_{n}^{p,q}
as

\nu_{n}^{p,q}=\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k(\ell+1)+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell+1)}f\mu(g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}),

and

g_{n}^{p,q}=\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}-\alpha=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}-\beta=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\varepsilon}h_{\alpha\beta}\mu(g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}),

where
\varepsilon=k(\ell+1)+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell+1)+(\alpha+\beta)(d_{1}+d_{2}+k+\ell)
and
d_{x}=p_{x}+q_{x}
for
x=1,2
. This expression will be used to prove the commutative version of the above lemma.

We now state the main result of this section.

.

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-103.62547pt}{-2.43054pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}};\,\text{ with }\partial{\bar{\partial}}=0\text{ on }H.

(2)

There is a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure on
(H,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
and a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence
g:H\rightsquigarrow A
.

Proof.

This is now a direct consequence of Proposition
2.23
together with Lemma
2.27
.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

Since a bba is, in particular, a bicomplex, we can consider its Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies. The product induces an associative algebra structure on the Bott-Chern cohomology and equips the Aeppli cohomology with the structure of a module over the Bott-Chern cohomology.

The bicomplex
H
of the above theorem is isomorphic to the ABC-cohomology

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)=H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\oplus\tilde{H_{\mathcal{A}}}(A),

see Section
2.1
. Consequently, the induced product does not make the ABC-cohomology into a bba. However, Theorem
2.29
provides a way to endow this cohomology with an algebra structure that is not strictly associative, but associative up to homotopy.

If the bba
A
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property, then the bicomplex
H
has trivial differentials. Hence, by Remark
2.16
, we have
\mu_{n}^{p,q}=0
whenever
p+q=2-n
. Moreover, in this case the bicomplex
H
is isomorphic to
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
, and the product
\mu_{2}
of Theorem
2.29
gives precisely the induced product on cohomology.

2.4.
Commutative algebras

We next define the analogues of commutative algebras in the setting of
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras, i.e.
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras, and we show that if we start with a commutative bidifferential bigraded algebra, then the formulas for the transferred
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure of Lemma
2.27
give a
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure.

To define
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-structures, we first recall the definition of shuffle permutations: A
(i,j)
-shuffle
is an element
\sigma
of the symmetric group
\mathbb{S}_{i+j}
such that

\sigma(1)<\sigma(2)<\dots<\sigma(i)\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma(i+1)<\sigma(i+2)<\dots<\sigma(i+j)

hold.

Denote by
\mathrm{Sh}_{i,j}
the set of
(i,j)
-shuffles. Define the element
\tau_{i,j}\in\mathbf{k}[\mathbb{S}_{i+j}]
by

\tau_{i,j}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}_{i,j}}\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\sigma.

Definition 2.31
.

A
pluripotential
C_{\infty}
-algebra
,
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra for short, is a
A_{\infty}^{pp}
-algebra such that

\mu_{i+j}^{p,q}\circ\tau_{i,j}=0

for every
i,j\geq 1
and every
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

Here,
\tau_{i,j}
acts on
A^{\otimes i+j}
by permuting the tensor factors. A
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra is then a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra satisfying commutativity relations. Likewise, a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism of
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras is required to satisfy the same commutativity relations.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
(A,\{\mu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
and
(B,\{\nu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
be two
C_{\infty}^{pp}
-algebras. A

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism of
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras

f:A\rightsquigarrow B
is a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism of
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras such that

f_{i+j}^{p,q}\circ\tau_{i,j}=0

for every
i,j\geq 1
and every
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

We next show that when the initial algebra is commutative, then the transferred structure of Lemma
2.27
is, in fact, a
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\mu)
be a commutative bidifferential bigraded algebra.
Given a contraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B,\partial,{\bar{\partial}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

the formulas defined in Lemma
2.27

\nu_{n}^{p,q}:=f\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{p,q}g^{\otimes n}\quad\text{ and }\quad g_{n}^{p,q}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i-\alpha=p\\
j-\beta=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(\alpha+\beta)(i+j+n)}h_{\alpha\beta}\mathfrak{m}_{n}^{i,j}g^{\otimes n},

for all
n\geq 2
, define a
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure on
B
and a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence of
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras
g\colon B\rightsquigarrow A
.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to the proof of Theorem 12 of
[
8
]
for
C_{\infty}
-algebras in the dg setting. We reproduce it here for the sake of completeness. By Lemma
2.27
,
\{\nu_{\bullet}^{*,*}\}
defines a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
B
and
g=(g,\{g_{\bullet}^{*,*}\})
defines a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-morphism. It just remains to prove the symmetric conditions

\nu_{i+j}^{p,q}\circ\tau_{i,j}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad g_{i+j}^{p,q}\circ\tau_{i,j}=0.

First of all, recall that, by Remark
2.28
, we can rewrite
\nu_{n}^{p,q}
and
g_{n}^{p,q}
as

\nu_{n}^{p,q}=\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k(\ell+1)+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell+1)}f\mu(g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}),

and

g_{n}^{p,q}=\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}-\alpha=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}-\beta=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k(\ell+1)+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell+1)+(\alpha+\beta)(d_{1}+d_{2}+k+\ell)}h_{\alpha\beta}\mu(g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}),

where
d_{x}=p_{x}+q_{x}
for
x=1,2
.

Let
\nabla_{2}:T(B)\to T^{2}(TB)
be the map given by

a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{n}\mapsto\sum_{k+\ell=n}(a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{k})\otimes(a_{k+1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{k+\ell}).

Define the shuffle product

\shuffle:T(B)\otimes T(B)\to T(B)

by

(a_{1},\dots,a_{i})\otimes(a_{i+1},\dots,a_{i+j})\mapsto\tau_{i,j}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{i+j}).

By Lemma 13 in
[
8
]
, we have

\nabla_{2}(TB\shuffle TB)\subset\tau_{1,1}(T^{2}(TB))\;\oplus\;TB\;\otimes\;(TB\shuffle TB)\;\oplus\;(TB\shuffle TB)\;\otimes\;TB.

We will show by induction on
n\geq 2
that
\nu_{n}^{p,q}
and
g_{n}^{p,q}
vanish on
TB\shuffle TB
, which implies
\nu_{i+j}^{p,q}\tau_{i,j}=0
and
g_{i+j}^{p,q}\tau_{i,j}=0
for any
i,j\geq 1
and any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

On the one hand, by induction hypothesis, the map

\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=i\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=j\end{subarray}}g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}

annihilates

TB\;\otimes\;(TB\shuffle TB)\;\oplus\;(TB\shuffle TB)\;\otimes\;TB.

On the other hand,

\sum_{k+\ell=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=i\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=j\end{subarray}}g_{k}^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes g_{\ell}^{p_{2},q_{2}}

takes the subspace
\tau_{1,1}T^{2}(TB)
to
\tau_{1,1}(T^{2}(A))
, but this is annihilated by
\mu
, and we conclude that
\nu_{n}^{p,q}(TB\shuffle TB)
and
g_{n}^{p,q}(TB\shuffle TB)
vanish.
∎

As a consequence, we obtain the following commutative version of Theorem
2.29
.

Theorem 2.34
.

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\mu)
be a commutative bidifferential bigraded algebra. There exists a minimal bicomplex
H
endowed with a
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-structure and a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence of
C^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebras
g\colon H\rightsquigarrow A
.

2.5.
Massey products in complex geometry

In their study of the topology of complex manifolds,
Angella and Tomasini
[
2
]
introduced triple Massey-type products for Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies (ABC-Massey products). These Massey products turn out to be obstructions to the notions of formality related to Bott-Chern and Aeppli cohomologies introduced in
[
24
]
. In this section, we review these concepts, present important examples and describe an explicit relation between these products and the
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra structure arising from the homotopy transfer theorem.

Let
(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}},\cdot)
be a bidifferential bigraded algebra. Then,
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
has an algebra structure induced by the product of the algebra and
H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)
has the structure of a
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}
-module.

We next recall the Aeppli-Bott-Chern-Massey (ABC-Massey) products defined in
[
2
]
.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
[x],[y],[z]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
be three Bott-Chern cohomology classes such that

[x]\cdot[y]=[y]\cdot[z]=0
. The
triple ABC-Massey product
\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle
is defined as the following set of Aeppli classes:

\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle:=\{[x\cdot v-u\cdot z]\,|\,\partial{\bar{\partial}}u=x\cdot y,\,\partial{\bar{\partial}}v=y\cdot z\}\subset H_{\mathcal{A}}(A).

The triple ABC-Massey product is said to be
trivial
if
0\in\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle
.

Let us remark that in
[
2
]
a different sign convention is used. We are sticking to the sign convention in
[
24
]
instead. Also, in these references, the triple product is defined as an element in the coset space

\frac{H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)}{{[x]\cdot H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)+[z]\cdot H_{\mathcal{A}}(A)}}

rather than as a set of Aeppli classes. Both viewpoints are equivalent. In particular, a triple ABC-Massey product is trivial if and only if it is zero in the above coset space.

When applied to the complexified de Rham algebra of a complex manifold, ABC-Massey products yield new holomorphic invariants with a homotopy–theoretic flavor. Moreover, in
[
24
]
, these Massey-type operations were shown to obstruct the notions of
strong
and
weak formality
associated with pluripotential weak equivalences.

Interestingly, ABC-Massey products need not vanish in many situations where ordinary
Massey products do.
The standard proof of formality for compact Kähler manifolds of
[
11
]

relies on the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property, but
Sferruzza and Tomassini
[
32
]
constructed an example of a compact manifold satisfying the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property while
admitting a nontrivial ABC-Massey product (see
Example
2.39
).
Subsequently,
[
26
]
showed that any closed Riemann surface of genus at least two
carries a nontrivial ABC-Massey product.
In particular, even compact Kähler manifolds may exhibit such nontrivial ABC-Massey products.
The examples in
[
26
]
do not have restricted holonomy, leaving open the question of
whether Calabi-Yau or hyperkähler manifolds are strongly formal. This was answered negatively for the Calabi-Yau case in the recent work
[
23
]
.

There are further contexts in which ABC-Massey products have been employed to obstruct
the existence of certain metrics, for example in
[
33
]
,
[
34
]
, and
[
35
]
.
Additional results on triple ABC-Massey products may be found in
[
39
]
,
[
7
]
, and

[
29
]
.

In
[
24
]
, Milivojević and Stelzig introduce higher Aeppli-Bott-Chern Massey products, extending the notion of triple ABC-products, and prove that are invariant under pluripotential weak equivalences. These higher ABC-Massey products take inputs and land in non-standard cohomology groups. Indeed, the possible targets of these products involve cohomology groups of the Bigolin complex (
[
4
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
31
]
), and their input and landing spaces depend on auxiliary choices. Moreover, they are not very accessible or computable in practice.

In contrast to these partially defined products, the homotopy transfer theorem for bidifferential bigraded algebras provides a robust homotopical framework for understanding higher products in cohomology.
More precisely, a minimal bicomplex (for instance, the
H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}
-cohomology of a bba) can
be endowed with a
A^{pp}_{\infty}
-algebra structure that encodes the full homotopy type of the original algebra.
In practice, this yields genuine operations, all acting on a single (typically small) space, which collectively capture all the relevant homotopical information. Moreover,
[
9
]
presents a package (
[
15
]
) for computing such arity-3 products (and which can be extended to higher arities), demonstrating that these operations are indeed computable.

We now explain the relation between triple ABC-Massey products and a transferred structure. Using Proposition
2.23
, given a contraction into a minimal bicomplex
(H,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
, we may define a new contraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A),\partial,{\bar{\partial}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
f:A\twoheadrightarrow H\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
and
g:H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}H\hookrightarrow A
.

Applying the pluripotential homotopy transfer theorem
(Theorem
2.29
) to this contraction yields an
A_{\infty}^{pp}
-structure on

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
together with a

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pp}}{{\infty}}

-weak equivalence

H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\rightsquigarrow A.

By Theorem
6.14
, the transferred structure on
H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
encodes the pluripotential homotopy type of
A
.

From now on, we will write
\mu_{3}:=\mu_{3}^{\text{-}1,\text{-}1},
for the transferred arity 3 operation of bidegree
(-1,-1).

Proposition 2.36
.

Given

[x],[y],[z]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
such that

[x]\cdot[y]=[y]\cdot[z]=0
and a contraction
(f,g,h)
as
2.5
, we have

-[g\mu_{3}([x],[y],[z])]_{\mathcal{A}}\in\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle,

where
[-]_{\mathcal{A}}
denotes taking the Aeppli cohomology class.

Proof.

Note first that there is an inclusion
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)\subseteq H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
, so we may view such classes in ABC-cohomology. Write
x=g([x])
,
y=g([y])
and
z=g([z])
. Since

[x]\cdot[y]=[y]\cdot[z]=0
, we may choose an element
u
such that

\partial{\bar{\partial}}u=x\cdot y\text{ and }h(x\cdot y)=-u.

In the same way, we can choose
v
such that

\partial{\bar{\partial}}v=y\cdot z\text{ and }h(y\cdot z)=-v.

This gives the following

\mu_{3}([x],[y],[z])=f(h(x\cdot y)\cdot z-x\cdot h(y\cdot z))=f(-u\cdot z+x\cdot v).

Thus, we obtain

[g\mu_{3}([x],[y],[z])]_{\mathcal{A}}=[x\cdot v-u\cdot z]_{\mathcal{A}}\in\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle.\qed

Corollary 2.37
.

Let

[x],[y],[z]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(A)
such that

[x]\cdot[y]=[y]\cdot[z]=0
. If

\mu_{3}([x],[y],[z])=0
then the triple ABC-Massey product
\langle[x],[y],[z]\rangle

is trivial.

Remark 2.38
.

This reflects the situation observed in the ordinary case. Indeed, the triple products of
A_{\infty}
-structures arising from homotopy transfer theory always detect Massey products. That is,

\pm\mu_{3}(a,b,c)\in\langle a,b,c\rangle.

Consequently, if
\mu_{3}(a,b,c)=0
, the corresponding Massey product must also vanish.

In
[
6
]
, an explicit relationship is established between
A_{\infty}
-structures and ordinary higher Massey products.
We expect that an analogous phenomenon should hold for pluripotential
A_{\infty}
-algebras and higher
ABC-Massey products.

The following example exhibits the phenomenon described in Proposition
2.36
.
The computations presented here are extracted from
[
9
]
and use the Sage package
[
15
]
.

Example 2.39
.

The following procedure, explained in
[
32
]
, gives a compact
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-manifold by constructing an orbifold of global quotient type out of the Iwasawa nilmanifold.

Consider the action
\sigma\colon\mathbb{C}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{3}

given by
\sigma(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(iz_{1},iz_{2},-z_{3})
.
This gives a well-defined action on the Iwasawa manifold. The resulting quotient

M=\mathbb{I}_{3}/\langle\sigma\rangle
is a compact orbifold with 16 isolated singular points.
By Theorem 7.1 of
[
32
]
, the orbifold
M
admits a resolution into a smooth
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-compact manifold.
The sub-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(M)
of
\mathcal{A}(\mathbb{I}_{3})
of differential forms which are left invariant for the action of
\mathbb{H}(\mathbb{C})
and
\sigma
-invariant is generated by the elements

a\overline{a},b\overline{b},c\overline{c},a\overline{b},b\overline{a},c\overline{a}\overline{b},ab\overline{c},abc,\overline{a}\overline{b}\overline{c}.

The only non-trivial differentials being

\partial(c\overline{c})=-ab\overline{c},\,\partial(c\overline{a}\overline{b})=-ab\overline{a}\overline{b}

and their complex conjugates.
This algebra satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-condition and so in particular, all cohomologies (including Dolbeault, Bott-Chern, Aeppli and ABC) coincide. In Figure
1
it is indicated a basis for the cohomology in each bidegree.

[\overline{abc}]

[abc\overline{abc}]

[ac\overline{ac}]
,
[ac\overline{bc}]
,

[bc\overline{ac}]
,
[bc\overline{bc}]

[a\overline{a}]
,
[a\overline{b}]
,

[b\overline{a}]
,
[b\overline{b}]

1

[abc]

Figure 1.
A basis for the cohomology of
M
.

In
[
32
]
, a non-trivial ABC-Massey product is computed on
M
:

\langle[a\overline{a}],[b\overline{b}],[b\overline{b}]\rangle_{ABC}\in\frac{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{2,2}(M)}{[a\overline{a}]\cdot H_{\mathcal{A}}^{1,1}(M)+[b\overline{b}]\cdot H_{\mathcal{A}}^{1,1}(M)},

which is represented by the nonzero Aeppli cohomology class
[bc\overline{b}\overline{c}]\in H_{A}^{2,2}(M).

We define contraction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma},\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}),\partial,{\bar{\partial}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

as follows:
the map
g
is induced by the identity on all elements. The map
f
is induced by the identity, except for the following elements, which belong to a square and for which
f
is zero:

\textstyle{c\overline{a}\overline{b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{ab\overline{a}\overline{b}}
\textstyle{c\overline{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{ab\overline{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The homotopy
h
is given by

h(ab\overline{a}\overline{b})=c\overline{c}

and zero otherwise.

Applying the homotopy transfer theorem we obtain that the
\mu_{3}
-product is non-trivial only when all 3 inputs have bidegree
(1,1)
. We obtain

\mu_{3}([a\overline{a}],[b\overline{b}],[b\overline{b}])=[b\overline{b}c\overline{c}]

which gives precisely the ABC-Massey product computed in
[
32
]
.

3.
Basics in operad theory

In this section, we recall the definitions of operads and operadic algebras, as well as the dual notions of cooperads and coalgebras, along with some important results, adapted to the pluripotential setting when needed. Most of the material presented here is adapted from
[
16
]
,
[
19
]
, and
[
14
]
. In our case, we will work over the closed symmetric monoidal model category of bicomplexes
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
over a field
\mathbf{k}
of characteristic zero, although most of the constructions are valid over any symmetric monoidal category.

3.1.
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules

For multiple constructions in operad theory,
it is convenient to work with the category
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
of
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules or, more generally, with
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}
, the category of differential graded
\mathbb{S}
-modules. In our context, we will consider bidifferential bigraded
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules. We next review their basic properties and present the Schur functor, a key ingredient to define operadic algebras.

Definition 3.1
.

A
bidifferential bigraded
\mathbb{S}\,
-module
(
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module for short)
M
is given by a sequence of bicomplexes
\{M(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
together with a right
\mathbb{S}_{n}
action, where
\mathbb{S}_{n}
denotes the
n
th symmetric group.

If
\mu\in M(n)^{p,q}
, we say that
\mu
is of
arity

n
and bidegree
(p,q)
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
morphism of
bb-
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules

f:M\to N
is a sequence of equivariant bicomplex morphisms
f(n):M(n)\to N(n)
.

We denote the category of bb-
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules by
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note we can view every
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
M
as a family of bicomplexes
\{M(n)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
where each
M(n)^{p,q}
is a right
\mathbf{k}[\mathbb{S}_{n}]
-module for all
(p,q)
.

Let
M
and
N
be two
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-modules. Define their
tensor product
as

(M\otimes N)(n):=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{S}_{p}\times\mathbb{S}_{q}}^{\mathbb{S}_{n}}M(p)\otimes N(q).

Here
\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{S}_{p}\times\mathbb{S}_{q}}^{\mathbb{S}_{n}}
denotes the induced
\mathbb{S}_{n}
-representation. That is,

\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{S}_{p}\times\,\mathbb{S}_{q}}^{\mathbb{S}_{n}}M(p)\otimes N(q)=\left(M(p)\otimes N(q)\right)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{p}\times\mathbb{S}_{q}}}\mathbf{k}[\mathbb{S}_{n}].

The
composition product
is given by

M\circ N=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}M(n)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}N^{\otimes n}.

Explicitly, the
n
-ary component of the composition is:

(M\circ N)(n)=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}M(k)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{k}}}\left(\bigoplus_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=n}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{S}_{i_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{S}_{i_{k}}}^{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\left(N(i_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes N(i_{k})\right)\right)

where the action of
\mathbb{S}_{k}
is given by permuting the factors in the tensor product

N(i_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes N(i_{k})

via permutation of the indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
.
Therefore, an element of
(M\circ N)(n)
is a linear combination of tuples

(\mu;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k})\sigma

where
\mu\in M(k)
,
\mu_{j}\in N(i_{j})
,
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
and
i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}=n
. Such tuples are subject to the following relations:

(1)

(\mu\tau;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k})=(\mu;\mu_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau^{-1}(k)})\tau_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}},

where
\tau_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}
is the block permutation of
\{1,\dots,n\}
that divides it into
k
blocks of sizes
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
, and permutes these blocks according to
\tau
and

(2)

(\mu;\mu_{1}\tau_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\tau_{k})=(\mu;\mu_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\dots,\mu_{\tau^{-1}(k)})\tau_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup\tau_{k},

where each
\tau_{j}\in\mathbb{S}_{i_{j}}
, and
\tau_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\tau_{k}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
is the permutation that acts within each block of size
i_{j}
by
\tau_{j}
.

To a bb-
\mathbb{S}\,
-module
M
we may associate an endofunctor in
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
called the
Schur functor

M:\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}

given by

M(A)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}M(n)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}A^{\otimes n}.

This functor satisfies

(M\otimes N)(-)\cong M(-)\otimes N(-)\text{ and }(M\circ N)(-)\cong M(N(-))

(see for instance Section 5.1 of
[
19
]
, or Section 1.2 in
[
16
]
). Moreover, we have:

Proposition 3.4
.

The triple

(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo},\otimes,\mathrm{id})
is a symmetric monoidal category with
\mathrm{id}=(\mathbf{k},0,\dots)
.
The triple

(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo},\circ,\mathrm{I})
is a monoidal category, with
\mathrm{I}=(0,\mathbf{k},0,\dots)
.

3.2.
Operads and operadic algebras

In this section, we recall the definitions of operads and operadic algebras along with some useful results, adapted to the pluripotential setting when needed.

Definition 3.5
.

An
operad
is a monoid in the monoidal category
(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo},\circ,\mathrm{I})
, that is, a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
\mathcal{P}
together with morphisms

\gamma:\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}\quad\text{ and }\quad\eta:\mathrm{I}\to\mathcal{P}

called the
composition
and
unit
maps, respectively, satisfying associativity

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P})\circ\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ(\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

and unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}\circ\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\circ\mathrm{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\circ\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

conditions.

In particular, the operad structure is determined by maps

\mathcal{P}(k)\otimes\mathcal{P}(i_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{P}(i_{k})\to\mathcal{P}(i_{1}+\dots+i_{k})

subject to associativity, unitality and equivariance conditions. Moreover, the differentials of
\mathcal{P}
,
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
, are
derivations
for
\gamma

\displaystyle\partial\big(\gamma(\mu;\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k})\big):=\gamma\big(\partial(\mu);\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\epsilon_{i}}\gamma\big(\mu;\mu_{1},\dots,\partial(\mu_{i}),\dots,\mu_{k}\big),

where
\epsilon_{i}=|\mu|+|\mu_{1}|+\dots+|\mu_{i-1}|
and
|-|
stands for the total degree. The same identity holds for
{\bar{\partial}}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad and let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(m)
an element in arity
m
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and element in arity
n
. We will sometimes use the
partial composition

(\mu,\nu)\;\longmapsto\;\mu\circ_{i}\nu\in\mathcal{P}(m-1+n)

defined for
1\leq i\leq m
by:

-\!\circ_{i}\!-\;:\;\mathcal{P}(m)\otimes\mathcal{P}(n)\;\longrightarrow\;\mathcal{P}(m-1+n),\qquad\mu\circ_{i}\nu\;:=\;\gamma\bigl(\mu;\;\underbrace{\mathrm{id},\dots,\mathrm{id}}_{i-1},\nu,\underbrace{\mathrm{id},\dots,\mathrm{id}}_{m-i}\bigr).

Definition 3.6
.

A
morphism of operads

f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is
a morphism of bb-
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules which is compatible with the composition and unit maps.

We denote the category of operads in
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
by
\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{BiCo})
.

Example 3.7
.

Let
A
be a bicomplex. Consider the internal
\hom
defined in Section
2.1

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(A,B)^{p,q}=\prod\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}(A^{i,j},B^{i+p,j+q}),

with differentials

\partial f:=[\partial,f]\quad\text{ and }\quad{\bar{\partial}}f:=[{\bar{\partial}},f].

Define the following bb-
\mathbb{S}\,
-module

\mathcal{E}nd(n):=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(A^{\otimes n},A)

where the action of
\mathbb{S}_{n}
is induced by the left
\mathbb{S}_{n}
action on
A^{\otimes n}
. We can endow it with an operadic structure given by composition of endomorphisms

(f;f_{1},\dots,f_{k}):=f(f_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes f_{k}).

The resulting operad is called the
endomorphism
operad.

If
\mathcal{P}
is an operad, then Proposition
3.4
shows that the Schur functor

\mathcal{P}:\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}

defines a monad.

Definition 3.8
.

A
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
is an algebra over the monad
\mathcal{P}
, that is, an object
A
in
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
together with maps of bicomplexes
\gamma_{A}\colon\mathcal{P}(A)\to A
and
\eta_{A}\colon A\to\mathcal{P}(A)
such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(\gamma_{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}

The
\mathcal{P}
-algebra structure is determined by maps

\gamma_{n}:\mathcal{P}(n)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}A^{\otimes n}\to A.

One should think of
\mathcal{P}(n)
as the space that parametrizes all the possible ways of combining
n
elements of the
\mathcal{P}
-algebra.

Using the fact that there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}(\mathcal{P}(n),\mathrm{Hom}(A^{\otimes n},A))\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{P}(n)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}A^{\otimes n},A),

one can alternatively define an algebra over an operad
\mathcal{P}
as a morphism of operads

\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{E}nd(A).

The image of an element
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(n)
is an operation
\mu_{n}:A^{\otimes n}\to A
. We denote the category of
\mathcal{P}
-algebras by
\mathcal{P}\text{-}\mathrm{Alg}
.

The following definition will be used to twist differentials on a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra.

Definition 3.9
.

A
derivation
of a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
A
is a linear map
d:A\to A
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ^{\prime}d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A}}

where
\mathrm{id}\circ^{\prime}d
denotes the map given by
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{id}^{i}\otimes d\otimes\mathrm{id}^{n-i}
in
\mathcal{P}(n)\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}A^{\otimes n}
.

If
A
is a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra, the bigraded vector space of derivations of
A
,

\mathrm{Der}(A)
,
is a bidifferential bigraded Lie algebra, with bracket the graded commutator and differentials the graded commutators with the differentials of
A
.

Definition 3.10
.

A
pair of differentials
of a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra consists in two derivations
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
of degree
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively, such that

(\partial+\tau)^{2}=0,\quad({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})^{2}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad(\partial+\tau)({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})+({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})(\partial+\tau)=0.

If
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
is a pair of differentials, then the bicomplex

(A,\partial+\tau,{\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})
is a
\mathcal{P}
-algebra with the same structure map
\gamma:\mathcal{P}(A)\to A
as
A
.

Since an operad
\mathcal{P}
is a monad, we have the definition of a free
\mathcal{P}
-algebra.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
A
be a bicomplex. The
free
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
over
A
is the algebra given by
\mathcal{P}(A)
itself, and the algebra structures
\gamma:\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}(A)\to\mathcal{P}(A)
and
\eta:\mathcal{P}(A)\to\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}(A)
are given by the monad structure of
\mathcal{P}
.

For any
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
A
and any linear map
f:V\to A
, there is a
unique
\mathcal{P}
-algebra extension
\tilde{f}:\mathcal{P}(V)\to A
of
f
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

Note that a free algebra is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

The following proposition is very useful for constructing derivations on free algebras; see for instance Section 6.3.6 in
[
19
]
,
[
16
]
, among others.
