Proposition 3.12
.

Any derivation
d
on a free
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
\mathcal{P}(A)
is uniquely determined by its restriction to the generators, that is, by a linear map
A\to\mathcal{P}(A)
.

More explicitly, given a map
\varphi:A\to\mathcal{P}(A)
, the unique
derivation
d_{\varphi}
of the free
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
\mathcal{P}(A)
extending

\varphi
is defined by

d_{\varphi}=(\gamma_{(1)}\circ\mathrm{id}_{A})\,(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}}\circ^{\prime}\varphi).

An algebra is said to be
quasi-free
if when we forget the differentials it is a free algebra.

There is a similar story in the non-symmetric setting. In this case, one considers sequences
M=\{M(n)\}_{n\geq 0}
of bicomplexes without the
\mathbb{S}_{n}
-action. The associated functor is defined by

M(A)=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}M(n)\otimes A^{\otimes n}.

All the constructions presented above admit an obvious non-symmetric analoge.

3.3.
The free operad

The
free operad
over a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
M
is an operad
\mathbb{T}(M)
with a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module morphism
\eta(M):M\to\mathbb{T}(M)
such that given an operad
\mathcal{P}
, any map
f:M\to\mathcal{P}
of
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-modules, extends uniquely into an operad morphism
\tilde{f}:\mathbb{T}(M)\to\mathcal{P}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}

The functor
\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}\to\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{BiCo})
is the left adjoint to the forgetful functor
U:\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{BiCo})\to\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}
. Arity-wise it can be computed as

\mathbb{T}(M)(n):=\mathop{\mathrm{colim}}_{T\in\mathrm{T}(n)}M(T),

where
\mathrm{T}(n)
denotes the set of rooted trees with
n
leaves. For a tree
T
, the object
M(T)
is the
treewise tensor product
of the

\mathbb{S}
-module
M
along the vertices of
T
:

M(T)=\bigotimes_{v\in V(T)}M(\mathrm{in}(v)),

where
\mathrm{in}(v)
denotes the number of inputs of the vertex
v
.
For details, see Section 5.6.1 of
[
19
]
or
[
16
]
.

Remark 3.13
.

When
M(0)=0
,

\mathbb{T}(M)(n)=\bigoplus_{T\in\mathrm{T}(n)}M(T).

It is helpful to think of an element of
\mathbb{T}(M)(n)
as a sum of rooted trees in which each vertex
v
is decorated by an element of
M(\mathrm{in}(v))
and each leaf is decorated by an element of
\{1,\dots,n\}
. The operad structure of the
\mathbb{S}\,
-module
\mathbb{T}(M)
is given by grafting of trees.

The following property will be very useful.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
M
be a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module. Any derivation
d_{\phi}:\mathbb{T}(M)\to\mathbb{T}(M)
of the free operad
\mathbb{T}(M)
is completely characterized by the image on the generators:
\phi:M\to\mathbb{T}(M)
.

When representing an element of
\mathbb{T}(M)
by a labeled tree, its image under
d_{\phi}
is the sum of labeled trees, where
\phi
has been applied once and only once to every vertex. Such operads are called
quasi-free
operads. It means that the underlying bigraded operad, forgetting the differentials, is free.

Remark 3.15
.

Let
M
be a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module, the free operad on
M
,
\mathbb{T}(M)
, has a weight-grading.
By definition, the
weight

w(\mu)
of an operation
\mu\in\mathbb{T}(M)

is defined as follows:

w(\mathrm{id})=0,\qquad w(\mu)=1\ \text{when}\ \mu\in M,

and

w(\nu;\,\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})=w(\nu)+w(\nu_{1})+\cdots+w(\nu_{n}).

We denote by
\mathbb{T}(M)^{(r)}
the
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module of operations of weight
r
.

A completely analogous construction exists for the free non-symmetric operad using planar trees instead, see
[
19
, Sections 5.9.5 and 5.9.6]
for details.

3.4.
Cooperads and coalgebras over cooperads

We next review the dual notions of operads and algebras, that is, cooperads and coalgebras.

Definition 3.16
.

A
cooperad
is a comonoid in the monoidal category
(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo},\circ,\mathrm{I})
. Explicitly, this consists of a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
\mathcal{C}
equipped with morphisms

\Delta:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}\quad\text{ and }\quad\varepsilon:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{I},

satisfying coassociativity and counit axioms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C})\circ\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ(\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{I}\circ\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ\mathrm{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\circ\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\circ\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ\varepsilon}

We denote the category of cooperads by
\mathrm{CoOp(\mathrm{BiCo})}
. We will sometimes adopt the following notation

\Delta(\mu)=\sum(\nu;\,\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{k})\sigma.

And sometimes use the shorter notation

\Delta(\nu)=\sum\nu_{(1)}\circ\nu_{(2)},

where

\nu_{(2)}=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{k})\sigma\in\mathcal{C}^{\otimes k}(n).

The differentials
\partial_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{C}}
are coderivations, under this notation, this means

\Delta\partial_{\mathcal{C}}(c)=\sum(\partial_{\mathcal{C}}(c)\,;\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\sigma+\sum\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\theta_{i}}\,(c\,;\,c_{1},\ldots,\partial_{\mathcal{C}}(c_{i}),\ldots,c_{k})\sigma,

where
\theta_{i}=|c|+|c_{1}|+\cdots+|c_{i-1}|
.

Given a cooperad
(\mathcal{C},\Delta,\eta)
, one may isolate from the full
decomposition map
\Delta:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}
the part that
corresponds to decomposing into two parts. Let
M
and
N
be two
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-modules, we denote by

M\circ_{(1)}N

the
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module defined in arity
n
by

(M\circ_{(1)}N)(n)\;=\;\bigoplus_{k+\ell=n+1}M(k)\otimes_{\mathbb{S}_{k}}\left(\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq k}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\times(j-1)}\times\mathbb{S}_{\ell}\times\mathbb{S}_{1}^{\times(k-j)}}^{\mathbb{S}_{n}}\mathrm{I}^{\otimes j-1}\otimes N(\ell)\otimes\mathrm{I}^{k-j}\right).

This yields the

infinitesimal decomposition map
of
\mathcal{C}
, defined as the
composition

\Delta_{(1)}\colon\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\;\Delta\;}\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathcal{C}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{C}.

The infinitesimal decomposition may be written in the form

\Delta_{(1)}(\nu)=\sum_{i}(\nu_{i}^{\prime}\circ_{e_{i}}\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime})\,\tau_{i},

for

\nu_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(k^{\prime}_{i})
,

\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(k^{\prime\prime}_{i})
,
and
\tau_{i}\in\mathbb{S}_{n}
,
where

\nu_{i}^{\prime}\circ_{e_{i}}\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\nu_{i}^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{id}^{e_{i}-1}\otimes\nu_{i}^{\prime\prime}\otimes\mathrm{id}^{k_{i}^{\prime}-e_{i}}).

If
\mathcal{C}
is a cooperad, then the Schur functor is a comonad, see
[
16
]
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an operad. A
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
is a coalgebra over the comonad
\mathcal{C}
. That is, an object
C
in
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
together with maps of bicomplexes
\Delta_{C}:C\to\mathcal{C}(C)
and
\varepsilon:\mathcal{C}(C)\to C
satisfying the coassociativity and counit constraints:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\Delta_{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathcal{C}}(C)}

The following definition will be used to twist codifferentials on a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra.

Definition 3.18
.

A
coderivation
of a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra is a linear map
d\colon C\to C
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ^{\prime}d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{C}}

The bigraded vector space of coderivations on a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
C
,
\mathrm{Coder}(C)
, is a bidifferential bigraded Lie algebra with bracket the graded commutator and differentials the graded commutators with the differentials of
C
.

Definition 3.19
.

A
pair of codifferentials
of a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra consists in two coderivations
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
of degree
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively, such that

(\partial+\tau)^{2}=0,\quad({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})^{2}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad(\partial+\tau)({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})+({\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})(\partial+\tau)=0.

If
(C,\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
is a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra and
(\tau,\overline{\tau})
is a pair of codifferentials, then the bicomplex

(C,\partial+\tau,{\bar{\partial}}+\overline{\tau})
is a
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra with the same structure map

\Delta_{C}\colon C\to\mathcal{C}(C)
as
C
.

Since a cooperad
\mathcal{C}
is a comonad, there is the notion of cofree coalgebra. The
cofree
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
over a bicomplex
V
is the algebra given by
\mathcal{C}(V)
itself, and the coalgebra structures
\Delta_{C}\colon\mathcal{C}(V)\to\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}(V)
and
\eta_{C}\colon\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}(V)\to\mathcal{C}(V)
are given by the comonad structure of
\mathcal{C}
.

For any
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
C
and any linear map
f\colon C\to V
, there is a
unique
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra extension
\tilde{f}\colon C\to\mathcal{C}(V)
of
f
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Note that a cofree coalgebra is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

The following will be very useful to construct coderivations of cofree coalgebras.

Proposition 3.20
.

Any coderivation
d
on a cofree
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
\mathcal{C}(V)
is completely characterized by its projection onto the space of cogenerators
V
.
Explicitly, given a map
\varphi\colon\mathcal{C}(V)\to V
, the unique coderivation

d_{\varphi}
on the cofree
\mathcal{C}
-coalgebra
\mathcal{C}(V)
that extends
\varphi

is given by

d_{\varphi}\;=\;\bigl(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V}\,;\,\varphi)\bigr)\bigl(\Delta_{(1)}\circ\mathrm{id}_{V}\bigr)

where
\Delta_{(1)}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{C}
is the infinitesimal
decomposition map of the cooperad.
The map

\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V}\,;\,\varphi):(\mathcal{C}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{C})(V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(V)

acts by applying
\mathrm{id}_{V}
to the
V
-tensor factor and applying

\varphi
to the
\mathcal{C}(V)
-tensor factor.

The
cofree cooperad
over a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
M
is a cooperad
\mathbb{T}^{c}(M)
such that
for any
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{C}\to M
sending
\mathrm{id}
to
0
,
there exists a unique cooperad morphism

\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{T}^{c}(M)
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}^{c}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\varphi}}

The following property will be very useful to construct coderivations

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
M
be a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module. Any coderivation
d_{\phi}\colon\mathbb{T}(M)\to\mathbb{T}(M)
of the free operad
\mathbb{T}(M)
is completely characterized by the image to the cogenerators
\phi\colon\mathbb{T}(M)\to M
.

4.
Minimal models of operads

We prove the existence and uniqueness of minimal models for reduced operads in
\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}
with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences. The models we give are defined inductively on arity, in the spirit of Sullivan’s minimal models and following the approach of Markl–Shnider–Stasheff
[
21
]
.

4.1.
Basic pluripotential operad homotopy theory

We introduce principal extensions of operads in bicomplexes and show that these satisfy a lifting property with respect to pluripotential weak equivalences. We mainly adapt results from
[
21
]
,
[
28
]
and
[
10
]
on basic homotopy theory of operads, to the pluripotential case. In particular, we introduce a functorial path for operads in bicomplexes and study the associated notion of homotopy equivalence.

We will work with
reduced
operads, i.e. operads
\mathcal{P}
satisfying
\mathcal{P}(0)=0
and
\mathcal{P}(1)=\mathbf{k}
. Reduced operads are
augmented
, that is, there exists an operad morphism
\varepsilon:\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{I}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. Let
(\mathcal{P},\partial_{\mathcal{P}},{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}})
be a quasi-free operad,
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{T}(M)
where
M
is a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-mod with
M(0)=M(1)=0
. An
arity n principal extension
of
\mathcal{P}
is the quasi-free operad

\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E):=\mathbb{T}(M\oplus E),

where
E
is a bigraded
\mathbb{S}\,
-module concentrated in arity
n
and
d=(\partial+{\bar{\partial}}):E\to\ker((\partial_{\mathcal{P}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}})(n))
a map of degree
(1,0)+(0,1)
. The differentials on
\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E)
are determined by the differentials of
\mathcal{P}
,
d
and the Leibniz rule.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E)
be an arity
n
principal extension of a quasi-free operad
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{T}(M)
and let
\varphi:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
be a morphism of operads.
A morphism
\varphi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E)\to Q
extending
\varphi
is uniquely determined by a morphism of
\mathbb{S}\,
-modules
f\colon E\to Q(n)
satisfying
(\partial_{\mathcal{Q}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{Q}})f=\varphi d
.

Proof.

By the universal property of free operads, any such
\varphi^{\prime}
is determined by its restriction
\varphi^{\prime}_{|M\oplus E}
, and
\varphi^{\prime}_{|M}=\varphi
, set
f:=\varphi^{\prime}_{|E}
. We have
(\partial_{\mathcal{Q}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{Q}})f=(\partial_{\mathcal{Q}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{Q}})\varphi^{\prime}_{|E}=\varphi^{\prime}\circ d_{|E}=\varphi d.

∎

The following is a pluripotential version of Lemma 3.139 in
[
21
]
. We adapt the proof given in
[
10
]
.
In our current setting,
weak equivalences and surjections are given by arity-wise pluripotential weak equivalences and surjections respectively.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\iota\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E)
an arity
n
principal extension and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\sqcup_{d}\mathbb{T}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}}

a commutative diagram of operad morphisms, where
\omega
is a surjective pluripotential weak equivalence. Then, there exists an operad morphism
\psi^{\prime}
making both triangles commute.

Proof.

Consider the diagram of bigraded
\mathbf{k}
-vector spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z(C(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}}(n)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z(C(\omega(n)))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id^{\oplus 3}\oplus\omega(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where
Z(\_)
denotes the space of Bott-Chern cocycles,
C(\_)
denotes the cone of a map of bicomplexes defined in Section
2.1
, and
\lambda=(\varphi\circ\partial{\bar{\partial}},-\varphi\circ\partial,-\varphi\circ{\bar{\partial}},\psi_{|E})
. We first show this diagram is well defined. Recall that

C(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}}(n))=\mathcal{Q}(n)[-1,-1]\oplus\mathcal{Q}(n)[-1,0]\oplus\mathcal{Q}(n)[0,-1]\oplus\mathcal{Q}(n)

with differentials given by

\displaystyle\partial(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)

\displaystyle=(\partial a,-\partial a^{\prime},-\partial a^{\prime\prime}-a,\partial b+a^{\prime})

\displaystyle{\bar{\partial}}(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)

\displaystyle=({\bar{\partial}}a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime}+a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},{\bar{\partial}}b+a^{\prime\prime}).

In the same way,

C(\omega(n))=\mathcal{Q}(n)[-1,-1]\oplus\mathcal{Q}(n)[-1,0]\oplus\mathcal{Q}(n)[0,-1]\oplus\mathcal{R}(n)

with differentials given by

\displaystyle\partial(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)

\displaystyle=(\partial a,-\partial a^{\prime},-\partial a^{\prime\prime}-a,\partial b+\omega(a^{\prime})),

\displaystyle{\bar{\partial}}(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)

\displaystyle=({\bar{\partial}}a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime}+a,-{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},{\bar{\partial}}b+\omega(a^{\prime\prime})).

Let
(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)\in Z(C(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}}(n)))
, then
(\partial+{\bar{\partial}})(a)=0
,
{\bar{\partial}}(a^{\prime})=-\partial(a^{\prime\prime})=a
,
-(\partial+{\bar{\partial}})b=a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}
, and

(\mathrm{id}^{\oplus 3}\oplus\omega(n))(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},b)=(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},\omega(b))\in Z(C(\omega(n)))

because
-(\partial+{\bar{\partial}})\omega(b)=-\omega((\partial+{\bar{\partial}})b)=\omega(a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime})
. It is also clear that
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\subseteq Z(C(\omega(n)))
by Lemma
4.2
.

We now construct a map
\mu:E\to Z(C(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}}(n)))
. Since
\omega
is a
pluripotential weak equivalence, we have that
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\omega(n)))=0
(see Section
2.1
). Let
(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},c)\in Z(C(\omega(n))),
then there is an element
(e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},f)
such that
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},f)=(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},c)
, note that

\partial{\bar{\partial}}(e,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime},f)=(\partial{\bar{\partial}}e,\partial{\bar{\partial}}e^{\prime}-\partial e,\partial{\bar{\partial}}e^{\prime\prime}-{\bar{\partial}}e,\partial{\bar{\partial}}f+\omega(-{\bar{\partial}}e^{\prime}+\partial e^{\prime\prime}+e)).

Since
\omega(n)
is surjective, we may choose
f^{\prime}\in Q(n)
such that
\omega(f^{\prime})=f
. Consider the element
(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},\partial{\bar{\partial}}f^{\prime}-{\bar{\partial}}e^{\prime}+\partial e^{\prime\prime}+e)\in Z(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}}(n)).
It satisfies

(\mathrm{id}^{\oplus 3}\oplus\omega)(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},\partial{\bar{\partial}}f^{\prime}-{\bar{\partial}}e^{\prime}+\partial e^{\prime\prime}+e)=(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},c).

This proves
\mathrm{id}^{\oplus 3}\oplus\omega(n)
is surjective. Thus, we can construct
\mu=(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta)(e)
by choosing a preimage of
\lambda(e)
through
(\mathrm{id}^{\oplus 3}\oplus\omega(n))
. In particular, we obtain a map
\beta:E\to\mathcal{Q}(n)
such that
-(\partial+{\bar{\partial}})\beta(e)=(\alpha^{\prime\prime}+\alpha^{\prime})(e)=-\varphi(\partial+{\bar{\partial}})(e)
. According to Lemma
4.2
, we may obtain
\psi^{\prime}
extending
\varphi
from
\beta:E\to\mathcal{Q}(n)
. Moreover,
\omega\beta=\psi|_{E}
.
∎

Definition 4.4
.

We will say that an operad is
cofibrant
if is the colimit of a sequence of principal extensions starting from the initial operad
\mathrm{I}
.

The following lifting lemma justifies the above definition. Its proof follows by induction using
Lemma
4.3
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a cofibrant operad. For every diagram of operads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

in which
\omega
is a surjective pluripotential weak equivalence, there exists a morphism of operads
\varphi^{\prime}
making the diagram commute.

Remark 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad and
K
a commutative bb-algebra.
Then
\mathcal{P}\otimes K=\{\mathcal{P}(n)\otimes K\}_{n\geq 0}
has a natural operad structure given by
(p\otimes a)\circ_{i}(q\otimes b)=(-1)^{|a||q|}(p\circ_{i}q)\otimes(ab)
.

Consider the cbba
R=\mathbf{k}[t,\partial t,{\bar{\partial}}t,\partial{\bar{\partial}}t]=\Lambda(t,\partial t,{\bar{\partial}}t,\partial{\bar{\partial}}t),
where
|t|=(0,0)
.
We have the unit
\iota\colon\mathbf{k}\to R
and evaluations
\delta^{0}
and
\delta^{1}
at
t=0
and
t=1
, respectively.
These morphisms satisfy
\delta^{0}\circ\iota=\delta^{1}\circ\iota=1.

By
[
38
, Lemma 2.13]
,
\iota
is a pluripotential weak equivalence of cbba’s.

Definition 4.7
.

We define a
functorial path
in the category of operads as the functor

-\otimes R:\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{BiCo})\to\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{BiCo}),

given on objects by
\mathcal{P}[t,\partial t,{\bar{\partial}}t,\partial{\bar{\partial}}t]=\mathcal{P}\otimes R
and on morphisms by
\varphi\otimes\operatorname{id}_{R}
together with the natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}\otimes R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}

such that
\delta^{k}\circ\iota=\mathrm{id},
where
\delta^{k}=\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta^{k}\colon\mathcal{P}\otimes R\to\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathbf{k}=\mathcal{P}
and
\iota=\mathrm{id}\otimes\iota\colon\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathbf{k}\to\mathcal{P}\otimes R
.

Since
\iota\colon\mathbf{k}\to R
is a pluripotential weak equivalence, we have
\iota(n)\colon\mathcal{P}(n)=\mathcal{P}(n)\otimes\mathbf{k}\to\mathcal{P}(n)\otimes R
is also a pluripotential weak equivalence of bicomplexes and hence,
\iota\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}\otimes R
is a pluripotential weak equivalence of operads.
Note also that the path preserves weak equivalences. Also, the map
(\delta^{0},\delta^{1})\colon\mathcal{P}\otimes R\to\mathcal{P}
is surjective. The maps
\delta^{0}
and
\delta^{1}
are surjective pluripotential weak equivalences.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\varphi,\psi\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
be two morphisms of operads. A
homotopy from

\varphi
to
\psi
is given by a morphism of operads
H\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}\otimes R
such that
\delta^{0}\circ H=\varphi
and
\delta^{1}\circ H=\psi
. We use the notation
H\colon\varphi\simeq\psi
.

We say that
\varphi
and
\psi
are
homotopic
if there exists a homotopy
H\colon\varphi\simeq\psi
.

The homotopy relation is symmetric and reflexive.
Moreover, it is transitive whenever the source is a cofibrant operad. The proof of this last fact follows from standard homotopy theory arguments (see for instance
[
10
]
).
Given operads
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{P}
, with
\mathcal{O}
cofibrant, denote by
[\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P}]
the set of homotopy classes of morphisms of operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{P}
.
The lifting property of Proposition
4.5
gives:

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a cofibrant operad. Any pluripotential weak equivalence
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
of operads induces a bijection
\omega_{*}\colon[\mathcal{O},\mathcal{P}]\to[\mathcal{O},\mathcal{Q}]
.

4.2.
Minimal models

We next define minimal operads as cofibrant operads satisfying an additional decomposability condition on their differentials, and prove their existence and uniqueness.

Recall that the free operad is weight graded and that
\mathbb{T}(M)^{(r)}
denotes the
\mathbb{S}\,
-module of operations of weight
r
, see Remark
3.15
.

Definition 4.10
.

A
minimal
operad is a cofibrant operad
\mathcal{M}
such that the differentials are minimal, in the sense that
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(\mathcal{M})\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{(\geq 2)}
.

The following lemma is a key result for building minimal models using principal extensions, we will use the cone of a map of bicomplexes defined in Section
2.1
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\alpha:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{P}
be a morphism of operads where

•

\mathcal{M}
is a free operad,

•

\alpha:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{P}
is a pluripotential weak equivalence in arities
<n
.

Then, there is an arity
n
principal extension
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\sqcup_{d+\overline{d}}\mathbb{T}(E)

and a map of operads
\alpha^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}
such that it is a pluripotential weak equivalence for arities
<n
and

(1)

the bicomplex
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property,

(2)

if
C(\alpha(n))
already satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property, then
\alpha^{\prime}(n):\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n)\to\mathcal{P}(n)
is a pluripotential weak equivalence for arities
\leq n
.

Proof.

Consider the bigraded
\mathbb{S}\,
-mod
E:=H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n))),
where

C(\alpha(n))=\mathcal{M}(n)[-1,-1]\oplus\mathcal{M}(n)[-1,0]\oplus\mathcal{M}(n)[0,-1]\oplus\mathcal{P}(n)

denotes the cone of the morphism of bicomplexes
\alpha(n)
. Consider a section
s:=(d,-d_{1},-d_{2},f)
of the projection

Z(C(\alpha(n)))\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n))).

Note that
s(e)\in Z(C(\alpha(n)))
implies

(\partial d,\partial d_{1},\partial d_{2}-d,\partial f-\alpha d_{1})=0\Rightarrow\begin{cases}\partial d=0\\
\partial d_{1}=0\\
\partial d_{2}=d\\
\partial f=\alpha d_{1}\\
\end{cases}

and

({\bar{\partial}}d,{\bar{\partial}}d_{1}+d,{\bar{\partial}}d_{2},{\bar{\partial}}f-\alpha d_{2})=0\Rightarrow\begin{cases}{\bar{\partial}}d=0\\
{\bar{\partial}}d_{1}=-d\\
{\bar{\partial}}d_{2}=0\\
{\bar{\partial}}f=\alpha d_{2}\\
\end{cases}

This shows that
(d_{1}+d_{2})
defines a differential
\partial_{M^{\prime}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{M^{\prime}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}\sqcup_{d_{1}+d_{2}}\mathbb{T}(E)
and
f
extends
\alpha
by Lemma
4.2
, i.e., there exists
\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}
such that
\alpha^{\prime}_{|\mathcal{M}}=\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}{|E}=f
.

We now prove (
1
), that is we prove that
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property, which is equivalent to

(3)

\mathrm{id}_{*}:H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha^{\prime}(n)))\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(C(\alpha^{\prime}(n)))

being an isomorphism, see Section
2.1
.

It is enough to see that
\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha^{\prime}(n)))=0
, where we recall that

\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}:=\frac{\mathrm{Im}\partial\cap\ker{\bar{\partial}}+\mathrm{Im}{\bar{\partial}}\cap\ker\partial}{\mathrm{Im}\partial{\bar{\partial}}}.

We have

C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))\cong\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n)\oplus\mathcal{P}(n)=\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\otimes(\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus E)\oplus\mathcal{P}(n),

then, every element in
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
can be expressed as
(a+e,a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},c)
.
Let
x=[(a+e,a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},c)]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))),
the cycle condition gives

\begin{cases}\partial(a+e)={\bar{\partial}}(a+e)=0,\\
-\partial(a^{\prime}+e^{\prime})=0,\\
{\bar{\partial}}(a^{\prime}+e^{\prime})=a+e,\\
-\partial(a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime})=a+e,\\
{\bar{\partial}}(a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime})=0,\\
\partial c=-\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime}+e^{\prime}),\\
{\bar{\partial}}c=-\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}).\end{cases}

Which implies
e=0
, since
\partial(a^{\prime}+e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}(n)
. Graphically, this means

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{{\bar{\partial}}c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{a^{\prime}+e^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bullet^{\partial c}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

We want to show that if a representative of a Bott-Chern class of
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
is in
\mathrm{Im}\partial+\mathrm{Im}\partial
then it is in
\mathrm{Im}\partial{\bar{\partial}}
and thus, its class is trivial.
Suppose there is an element
(z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})\in C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
such that

\partial(z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})=(a,a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},c),

then

\begin{cases}\partial z=a,\\
-\partial z^{\prime}=a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},\\
-\partial z^{\prime\prime}-z=a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},\\
\partial c^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime}(z^{\prime})=c,\\
\end{cases}

which implies
e^{\prime}=0
since
\partial z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(n)
and
\partial{\bar{\partial}}z^{\prime}={\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime}=a
. Now, since
e^{\prime\prime}\in E=H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n)))
, we have, by definition, that

e^{\prime\prime}=[s(e^{\prime\prime})]=[(-\partial{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},\partial a^{\prime\prime}+a,{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},-{\bar{\partial}}c-\alpha(a^{\prime\prime}))].

Note that

(-{\bar{\partial}}z^{\prime}-a^{\prime\prime},0,0,c^{\prime})\in C(\alpha(n))

and

\begin{gathered}\partial{\bar{\partial}}(-a^{\prime\prime}-{\bar{\partial}}z^{\prime},0,0,c^{\prime})=(-\partial{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},\partial a^{\prime\prime}+\partial{\bar{\partial}}z^{\prime},{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},\partial{\bar{\partial}}c^{\prime}-\alpha(a^{\prime\prime})-\alpha({\bar{\partial}}z^{\prime}))=\\
=(-\partial{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},\partial a^{\prime\prime}+a,{\bar{\partial}}a^{\prime\prime},-{\bar{\partial}}c-\alpha(a^{\prime\prime}))=s(e^{\prime\prime}).\end{gathered}

This implies
e^{\prime\prime}=0
. Therefore,
x=[(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},c)]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n)))=E
and hence
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(x,0,0,0)=(a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime},c)
in
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
, indicating that
x
is trivial. A similar argument shows that if there is an element
(z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})\in C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
such that
{\bar{\partial}}(z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime},c^{\prime})=(a,a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},c),
then we also have that
x=[(a,a^{\prime}+e^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}+e^{\prime\prime},c)]
is trivial.
Thus, we can conclude
\widetilde{H}_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha^{\prime}(n)))=0
.

We now prove (
2
). Suppose

C(\alpha(n))
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property. We want to show
\alpha^{\prime}(n)
is a pluripotential weak equivalence for
m=0,\dots,n.
Since
\alpha^{\prime}(m)=\alpha(m)
for
m=0,\dots,n-1
, it is a pluripotential weak equivalence by induction hypothesis. Let us prove that

\alpha^{\prime}(n)=(\alpha\oplus f):\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n)=\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus E\to\mathcal{P}(n)

is a pluripotential weak equivalence.

Consider the map

j:\mathcal{M}(n)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n)=\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus E,

and the morphism

(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime}):C(j)=\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\otimes\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus(\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus E)\to C(\alpha(n))=\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\otimes\mathcal{M}(n)\oplus\mathcal{P}(n).

We will prove that
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})
and
H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})
are isomorphisms.

Let
[(x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},y+e)]\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(j))
, then

\begin{cases}{\bar{\partial}}x^{\prime}=x,\\
-\partial x^{\prime\prime}=x,\\
j(x^{\prime})=x^{\prime}=-\partial(y+e),\\
j(x^{\prime\prime})=x^{\prime\prime}=-{\bar{\partial}}(y+e).\end{cases}

Therefore,

\begin{gathered}=[(\partial{\bar{\partial}}(y+e),-\partial(y+e),-\partial(y+e),y+e)]=\\
=[(\partial{\bar{\partial}}e,-\partial e,-\partial e,e)]+[(\partial{\bar{\partial}}y,-\partial y,-{\bar{\partial}}y,y)]=\\
=[(\partial{\bar{\partial}}e,\partial e,-\partial e,e)]+[\partial{\bar{\partial}}(y,0,0,0)].\end{gathered}

This implies that every element in
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(j))
has a representative
[({\bar{\partial}}\partial e,\partial e,-{\bar{\partial}}e,e)]
, where
e\in E
. If
(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})(\partial{\bar{\partial}}e,-\partial e,-{\bar{\partial}}e,e)=(\partial{\bar{\partial}}e,-\partial e,-{\bar{\partial}}e,\alpha^{\prime}e)=s(e)=\partial{\bar{\partial}}(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime},c)
, then
e=0
. This proves
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})
is injective.

Let
e\in H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n)))
, we can choose a representative
e=[(x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},c)]
, which implies
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(e)=x
,
\partial(e)=-x^{\prime}
,
{\bar{\partial}}(e)=-x^{\prime\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}(e)=c
. Then

[(x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},e)]\mapsto[(x,x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},c)].

This proves
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})
is surjective.

Now, since the map
C(\alpha(n))
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property,
\mathrm{id}_{*}\colon H_{\mathcal{A}}(C(\alpha(n)))\to H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n)))
is an isomorphism. We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(C(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(\alpha(n)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(C(\alpha(n)))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{*}}

which implies
\mathrm{id}_{*}:H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(C(j))\to H_{\mathcal{A}}(C(j))
is injective and, hence, an isomorphism, see
2.1
. This gives us the isomorphism
H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})
.

By Lemma
2.2
, there is a commutative diagram, where the rows are exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(\mathcal{M}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(C(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(\mathcal{M}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(C(j))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(\mathcal{M}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(\mathcal{P}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}^{p-1,q-1}(C(\alpha(n)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(\mathcal{M}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(\mathcal{P}(n))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}^{pq}(C(\alpha(n)))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\oplus\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

by the Five Lemma,
H_{\mathcal{A}}(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
and
H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
are isomophisms, which shows that

\alpha^{\prime}(n)
is a pluripotential weak equivalence.
∎

Theorem 4.12

(Existence of minimal models)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an operad such that
\mathcal{P}(0)=0
and
\mathcal{P}(1)=\mathbf{k}
.
There exists a minimal operad
\mathcal{M}
and a pluripotential weak equivalence
\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

We will define, inductively on arity
m\geq 2
,
a morphism of operads

\alpha_{m}:\mathcal{M}_{m}\to\mathcal{P}

in such a way that:

(i)

\mathcal{M}_{m}
is a minimal operad generated in arities
<m
,

(ii)

\alpha_{m}\colon\mathcal{M}_{m}\to\mathcal{P}
is a pluripotential weak equivalence in arities
<m
.

For our base case, we begin by adding all zig-zags in arity 2:
let
E
be the
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
E=H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(2))
and
s_{2}:H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(2))\to\mathcal{P}(2)
a section of a projection
\mathcal{P}(2)\cong H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(2))\oplus\mathcal{P}(2)_{sq}\twoheadrightarrow H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(2))
. We set

\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathbb{T}(E),\quad\partial_{2_{|E}}=\partial_{H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}}\quad{\bar{\partial}}_{2_{|E}}={\bar{\partial}}_{H_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}}\quad\alpha_{2}:\mathcal{M}_{2}\to\mathcal{P},\;\alpha_{2_{|E}}=s_{2}.

Note that
\partial_{2_{|E}}{\bar{\partial}}_{2_{|E}}=0
. The map
\alpha_{2}
is a morphism of operads and is a pluripotential weak equivalence in arities
\leq 2
.

Assume we have defined

\alpha_{m}\colon\mathcal{M}_{m}\to\mathcal{P}
for all
m<n
. Let us now build
\alpha_{n}:\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{P}
. We apply Lemma
4.11
to construct an arity
n
principal extension
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{n-1}\sqcup_{d^{\prime}+\overline{d^{\prime}}}\mathbb{T}(E^{\prime})
and obtain a map
\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}
such that
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
satisfies the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property.

Then,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is minimal since it is cofibrant and
d_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=(\partial_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}})(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\prime(\geq 2)}
. Indeed,

•

d_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}|\mathcal{M}_{n-1}}=d_{\mathcal{M}_{n-1}}
, but
\partial_{\mathcal{M}_{n-1}},{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}_{n-1}}
are minimal by induction hypothesis,

•

d_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}|E^{\prime}}:E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{d^{\prime}+\overline{d}^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{n-1}(n)\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\prime(\geq 2)}.

We now apply again Lemma
4.11
to the map
\alpha^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{P}
, which satisfies that
C(\alpha^{\prime}(n))
has the
\partial{\bar{\partial}}
-property. This produces a map
\alpha_{n}\colon\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\sqcup_{d_{n}+\overline{d}_{n}}\mathbb{T}(E)\to\mathcal{P}
which is a pluripotential weak equivalence in arities
\leq n
.

Again,
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is minimal since it is cofibrant and
\partial_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}(\mathcal{M}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\geq 2)}
. Indeed,

•

(\partial_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}})_{|\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}=\partial_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
, but
\partial_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}},{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
are already minimal and

•

(\partial_{\mathcal{M}_{n}}{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{M}_{n}})_{|E}:E\xrightarrow{\overline{d}_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{n-1}(n)\oplus E^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\partial\oplus d^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{n-1}(n)\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\geq 2)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

A morphims
\phi\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
of operads between two minimal operads
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbb{T}(M),\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
and
\mathcal{N}=(\mathbb{T}(N),\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
is an isomorphism if and only if it is a pluripotential weak equivalence.

Proof.

Let us first introduce some notation.
Let
M=\{M(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
be a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module and let
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}(M)
. We will
denote
M_{<k}
the
\mathbb{S}\,
-module defined by

M_{<k}(n):=\begin{cases}M(n),\text{ if }k<n,\\
0,\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and
M_{\leq k}
the
\mathbb{S}\,
-module defined by

M_{\leq k}(n):=\begin{cases}M(n),\text{ if }k\leq n,\\
0,\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Finally,
\mathcal{M}_{\leq k}:=\mathbb{T}(M_{\leq k})
.

Now, one implication is clear since an isomorphism is already a weak equivalence. Let us prove the other implication: We will prove, by induction, that the restriction
\phi_{n}:\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}\to\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}
, is an isomorphism for each
n
. This will imply that
\phi:\mathbb{T}(M)\to\mathbb{T}(N)
is an isomorphism too.

Since
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbb{T}(M),\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{T}(N),\partial,{\bar{\partial}})
are minimal, we have that
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(\mathcal{M}(2))=0
and
\partial{\bar{\partial}}(\mathcal{N}(2))=0
. Since a pluripotential weak equivalence between minimal bicomplexes is an isomorphism,
\phi_{2}:\mathcal{M}(2)\to\mathcal{N}(2)
is an isomorphism. Now, suppose we have already proved that
\phi_{n}
is an isomorphism. We have two short exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1)\to\mathcal{M}(n+1)\to M(n+1)\to 0,

and

0\to\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}(n+1)\to\mathcal{N}(n+1)\to N(n+1)\to 0.

As a result, again by Lemma
2.2
, we obtain the following two commutative diagrams, where the rows are exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(M(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{A}}(N(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{N}(n+1))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(M(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}(\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}(\mathcal{M}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{N}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}}(N(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}(\mathcal{N}_{\leq n}(n+1))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{\mathcal{B}_{p,q}}(\mathcal{N}(n+1))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

by the Five Lemma, it follows that
\phi(n+1)\colon M(n+1)\to N(n+1)
is a pluripotenital weak equivalence.
The minimality of
\mathcal{M}
implies that the differentials
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
on

M(n+1)\cong\mathcal{M}(n+1)/\mathcal{M}_{\leq n}(n+1)

verify
\partial{\bar{\partial}}=0
, and the same holds true for
\mathcal{N}
. Again, this implies
\phi(n+1)\colon M(n+1)\to N(n+1)
is an isomorphism. We have proved that
\phi_{n+1}
induces an isomorphism on the generators, hence, the map

\phi_{n+1}:\mathbb{T}(M(\leq n+1))\to\mathbb{T}(N(\leq n+1))

is also an isomorphism.
∎

Definition 4.14
.

A
minimal model
of an operad
\mathcal{P}
in bicomplexes is given by a minimal operad
\mathcal{M}
together with a pluripotential weak equivalence
\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}
.

Theorem 4.15

(Uniqueness of minimal models)
.

Minimal models are unique up to isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}
and
\alpha^{\prime}:\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}
two minimal models of an operad
\mathcal{P}
. By Proposition
4.9
, there is a map
\phi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
unique up to homotopy such that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

We obtain
\alpha^{\prime}(n)\circ\phi(n)\simeq\alpha(n)
for every
n
and, by Lemma
2.7
,
\phi
is a pluripotential weak equivalence. By Proposition
4.13
,
\phi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is an isomorphism.
∎

5.
Koszul duality

In this section, we introduce the notion of a pluripotential twisting morphism and develop pluripotential Koszul duality for quadratic operads in
\mathrm{Vect}_{\mathbf{k}}
. To do so, we make use of the inflation functor defined in
[
20
]
, which allows us to use many of the computations in the dg-case.

5.1.
Twisting morphisms and twisted composite products

We introduce operadic twisting morphisms for operads in bicomplexes.
As in the classical setting, they play a central role in the bar-cobar constructions. We then use twisting morphisms to define twisted composite products, which lead to the notion of Koszul operads.

Let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{C}
be an operad and a cooperad in bicomplexes.
Consider the
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
given by

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})(n)^{p,q}:=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{C}(n),\mathcal{P}(n))^{p,q}

with right action of the symmetric group given by:

f^{\sigma}(x):=f(x^{\sigma^{-1}})^{\sigma}.

As in the dg setting, the pre-Lie product on
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
is defined as

(4)

\displaystyle f\star g:=\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\Delta_{(1)}}\mathcal{C}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{f\circ_{(1)}g}\mathcal{P}\circ_{(1)}\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{(1)}}\mathcal{P}.

The space of invariant elements of
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
under the symmetric action

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})

is stable under the pre-Lie product. The two components
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
are derivations with respect to the pre-Lie product
\star
(see
[
19
, Proposition 6.4.5]
), and we obtain

Proposition 5.1
.

The product space of
\mathbb{S}
-equivariant maps

\left(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P}),\star,\partial,{\bar{\partial}}\right)

is a bidifferential bigraded pre-Lie algebra.

Recall that a twisting morphism in the dg case is an equivariant map
\varphi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}
of degree
-1
satisfying

[d,\varphi]+\varphi\star\varphi=0.

With this in mind, we introduce the following bigraded version of a twisting morphism.

Definition 5.2
.

A
twisting morphism

\varphi=(\varphi^{10},\varphi^{01}):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}
consists of two maps
\varphi^{10},\varphi^{01}\in\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
of bidegrees
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively, satisfying:

\begin{cases}[\partial,\varphi^{10}]+\varphi^{10}\star\varphi^{10}=0,\\
[{\bar{\partial}},\varphi^{10}]+\varphi^{10}\star\varphi^{01}+[\partial,\varphi^{01}]+\varphi^{01}\star\varphi^{10}=0,\\
[{\bar{\partial}},\varphi^{01}]+\varphi^{01}\star\varphi^{01}=0.\end{cases}

Remark 5.3
.

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}
be an equivariant map such that it splits into two components of bidegrees
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
,
\varphi=\varphi^{10}+\varphi^{01}
. Then,
\varphi
is a twisting morphism in the dg sense with respect to the total differential
d=\partial+{\bar{\partial}}
if, and only if,
(\varphi^{10},\varphi^{01})
is a twisting morphism in the bigraded sense.

A
left module
over
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module
M
equipped with a left action

\lambda:\mathcal{P}\circ M\to M

which commutes with the differentials.

Let
N
be a
\mathrm{bb}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-module. The
free left
\mathcal{P}
module
on
N
is given by

(\mathcal{P}\circ N,\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ N},{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ N}),

where the action
\lambda
is given by the operadic composition.

Any linear map
\alpha:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}
from a cooperad
(\mathcal{C},\Delta,\epsilon)
to an operad
(\mathcal{P},\gamma,u)
, defines a map

\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathcal{C}\circ\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\alpha\circ\mathrm{id}}\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}

which, by Proposition 6.3.9 in
[
19
]
, induces a unique derivation on
\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}
, explicitly given by

d=(\gamma_{(1)}\circ\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})(\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}}\circ^{\prime}[(\alpha\circ\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}})\Delta].

Moreover, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})
, the following relation is satisfied

d_{\alpha\star\beta}=d_{\alpha}\circ d_{\beta},

see, for instance,
[
19
, Proposition 6.4.7]
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{P}
be a twisting morphism. Then, we define the
left twisted composite product
as

\mathcal{P}\circ_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}:=(\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C},\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{10}},{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{01}}).

Proposition 5.4
.

The differentials of the twisted composite product satisfy the bicomplex equations:

\begin{cases}(\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{10}})^{2}=0,\\
({\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{01}})^{2}=0,\\
(\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{10}})({\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{01}})+({\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{01}})(\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\alpha^{10}})=0.\end{cases}

Proof.

By Remark
5.3
,
\varphi=\varphi^{10}+\varphi^{01}
is a dg twisting morphism. We can endow
\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}
with a total differential of degree 1

d+d_{\varphi}=\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\varphi^{10}}+{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\varphi^{01}}.

Thus, we have
(d+d_{\varphi})^{2}=d_{[d,\varphi]+\varphi\star\varphi}
, which is zero because
\varphi
is a dg twisting morphism. Since
d+d_{\varphi}
splits into the two components

\partial_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\varphi^{10}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{C}}+d_{\varphi^{01}}

of bidegree
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, by degree reasons we obtain the desired result.
∎

5.2.
Stairway to heaven

We introduce a family of minimal bicomplexes, which will serve as a minimal generalization of the shift functor, and state some properties. This family is the key ingredient for constructing the inflation functor defined in
[
20
]
.

\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\ell}

of bidegree
(0,0)
for every
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
given by

\iota_{k,\ell}(p,q)_{k+\ell}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha+\delta=p\\
\beta+\gamma=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(\alpha+\beta)(\delta+\gamma+\ell)}(\alpha,\beta)_{k}\otimes(\delta,\gamma)_{\ell}.

The following is proved in
[
20
]

Proposition 5.7
.

The maps are coassociative in the sense that

(\iota_{k_{1},k_{2}}\otimes\mathrm{id})\iota_{k_{1}+k_{2},k_{3}}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\iota_{k_{2},k_{2}})\iota_{k_{1},k_{2}+k_{3}}

for any
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

We can define a generalized map

\iota_{k_{0},\dots,k_{r}}:\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{k_{0}+\dots+k_{r}}\to\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{k_{0}}\otimes\dots\otimes\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{k_{r}}

for
k_{0},\dots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
by recursively applying
\iota_{k,\ell}
which gives

\iota_{k_{0},\dots,k_{r}}(p,q)_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{0}+\dots+p_{r}=p\\
q_{0}+\dots+q_{r}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\varepsilon}(p_{0},q_{0})_{k_{0}}\otimes\dots\otimes(p_{r},q_{r})_{k_{r}},

where

\varepsilon:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(p_{i}+q_{i})\right)(p_{j}+q_{j}+k_{j})
and
n=k_{0}+\dots+k_{r}
.

It follows from the proof of Proposition 4.1 in
[
20
]
, for the case
r=2
, that:

Proposition 5.8
.

The maps
\iota_{k_{0},\dots,k_{r}}
are pluripotential weak equivalences for every
k_{0},\dots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
.

,

of bidegree
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively, for
n\leq-1
given by

\mathfrak{s}(p,q)_{n}=(-1)^{p+q+n}(p+1,q)_{n+1},\quad\overline{\mathfrak{s}}(p,q)_{n}=(-1)^{p+q+n}(p,q+1)_{n+1}.

Remark 5.9
.

These maps satisfy the following

\partial\mathfrak{s}+\mathfrak{s}\partial=0,\quad{\bar{\partial}}\overline{\mathfrak{s}}+\overline{\mathfrak{s}}{\bar{\partial}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial\overline{\mathfrak{s}}+{\bar{\partial}}\mathfrak{s}+\overline{\mathfrak{s}}\partial+\mathfrak{s}{\bar{\partial}}=0.

The maps
\iota
,
\mathfrak{s}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{s}}
satisfy compatibility conditions:

Proposition 5.10
.

The following hold

(1)

(\mathfrak{s}_{k-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\iota_{k-1,\ell}=(-1)^{k}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathfrak{s}_{\ell-1})\iota_{k,\ell-1}=\iota_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{s}_{k+\ell-1}
,

(2)

(\overline{\mathfrak{s}}_{k-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\iota_{k-1,\ell}=(-1)^{k}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\overline{\mathfrak{s}}_{\ell-1})\iota_{k,\ell-1}=\iota_{k,\ell}\overline{\mathfrak{s}}_{k+\ell-1}
.

Proof.

Let us prove (
1
), (
2
) is analogous. On the one hand, we have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{s}\otimes\mathrm{id})\iota_{k-1,\ell}(p,q)_{k+\ell-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{1}+k-1+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell)}(p_{1}+1,q_{1})_{k}\otimes(p_{2},q_{2})_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p+1\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{1}+k+d_{2}+\ell+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell)}(p_{1},q_{1})_{k}\otimes(p_{2},q_{2})_{\ell},

where
d_{i}=p_{i}+q_{i}
. On the other hand,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathfrak{s})\iota_{k,\ell-1}(p,q)_{k+\ell-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{2}+\ell-1+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell-1)+d_{1}}(p_{1},q_{1})_{k}\otimes(p_{2}+1,q_{2})_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p+1\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{1}+d_{2}+\ell+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell)}(p_{1},q_{1})_{k}\otimes(p_{2},q_{2})_{\ell}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\iota_{k,\ell}\mathfrak{s}(p,q)_{k+\ell-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}+p_{2}=p+1\\
q_{1}+q_{2}=q\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{1}+d_{2}+k+\ell+d_{1}(d_{2}+\ell)}(p_{1},q_{1})_{k}\otimes(p_{2},q_{2})_{\ell}.\qed

5.3.
The inflation functor

We next review the definition of the
inflation functor

constructed in
[
20
]
. It is a functor

\mathrm{Inf}:\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}_{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathord{\mspace{1mu}\text{\hskip 1.50694pt\hbox to3.31pt{\vbox to3.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.17 0 L 4.17 4.17 L 0 4.17 M 2.29 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mspace{1mu}}}

where
\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}
is the category of cochain complexes over
\mathbf{k}
concentrated in non-positive degrees and
\mathrm{BiCo}^{\mathord{\mspace{1mu}\text{\hskip 1.50694pt\hbox to3.31pt{\vbox to3.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.17 0 L 4.17 4.17 L 0 4.17 M 2.29 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mspace{1mu}}}
denotes the category of bicomplexes concentrated in the 3rd quadrant, that is
B^{p,q}=0
for
p>0
or
q>0
. Given a cochain complex concentrated in negative degrees
(C,d)

it is defined as follows:

\mathrm{Inf}(C)=\left(\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}}\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{n}\otimes C^{n},\partial,{\bar{\partial}}\right),

where we are considering
C^{n}
as a bicomplex sitting in degree
(0,0)
and
\hbox to6.49pt{\vbox to7.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.59 L 7.87 0.59}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 8.66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{n}
is the bicomplex defined in Section
5.2
. The differentials
\partial
and
{\bar{\partial}}
are defined by:

(5)

\partial((p,q)_{n}\otimes c)=q(p+1,q)_{n}\otimes c+(-1)^{p+q+n}(p+1,q)_{n+1}\otimes dc,

{\bar{\partial}}((p,q)_{n}\otimes c)=-p(p,q+1)_{n}\otimes c+(-1)^{p+q+n}(p,q+1)_{n+1}\otimes dc.

The inflation functor is symmetric colax monoidal, and maps quasi-isomorphisms to pluripotential weak equivalences (see
[
20
]
).

We now adapt the inflation functor to
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}
modules of cochain complexes and bicomplexes:

\mathrm{Inf}\colon\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}_{\mathbf{k}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\mathord{\mspace{1mu}\text{\hskip 1.50694pt\hbox to3.31pt{\vbox to3.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.17 0 L 4.17 4.17 L 0 4.17 M 2.29 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mspace{1mu}}}.

Let

\rho\colon\mathbb{S}_{n}\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(C),

be a right
\mathbb{S}_{n}
action on a cochain complex
C
. The inflation functor induces a group morphism

\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(C)\to\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathrm{Inf}(C))

given by

\mathrm{Inf}(f)((p,q)_{n}\otimes c)=(p,q)_{n}\otimes f(c).

By composition, we obtain a group morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathrm{Inf}(C))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

which defines a right action on
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathrm{Inf}(C))
.

Let
M
be in
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}
, then we define

\mathrm{Inf}(M)(n):=\mathrm{Inf}(M(n)).

The categories
(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0},\circ,\mathrm{I})
and
(\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}^{\mathord{\mspace{1mu}\text{\hskip 1.50694pt\hbox to3.31pt{\vbox to3.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.17 0 L 4.17 4.17 L 0 4.17 M 2.29 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mspace{1mu}}},\circ,\mathrm{I})
are monoidal. We have:

Proposition 5.11
.

The functor

\mathrm{Inf}\colon\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}\to\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{BiCo}^{\mathord{\mspace{1mu}\text{\hskip 1.50694pt\hbox to3.31pt{\vbox to3.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15pt\lower-0.15pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.17 0 L 4.17 4.17 L 0 4.17 M 2.29 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\mspace{1mu}}}

is colax monoidal. Moreover, for all
M
and
N
in
\mathbb{S}\,\text{-}\mathrm{Ch}^{\leq 0}
the comparison map

\mathrm{Inf}(M\circ N)\to\mathrm{Inf}(M)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(N)

is a weak equivalence, i.e. an arity-wise pluripotential weak equivalence.

Proof.

We will construct natural equivariant maps

\psi_{M,N}(n):\mathrm{Inf}(M\circ N)(n)\to(\mathrm{Inf}(M)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(N))(n)

for any
\mathrm{dg}\text{-}\mathbb{S}\,
-modules
M
and
N
in non-positive degrees,
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Inf}\big((M\circ N)\circ P\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Inf}\big(M\circ(N\circ P)\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Inf}(M\circ N)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Inf}(M)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(N\circ P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big(\mathrm{Inf}(M)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(N)\big)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Inf}(M)\circ\big(\mathrm{Inf}(N)\circ\mathrm{Inf}(P)\big)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{M\circ N,P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{M,N\circ P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{M,N}\circ\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\circ\psi_{N,P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and satisfying the unitality conditions
with respect to
\mathrm{id}:\mathrm{Inf}(\mathrm{I})=\mathrm{I}\to\mathrm{I}
. The horizontal maps in the above square are induced by the symmetric monoidal structures on cochain complexes and bicomplexes, respectively.

First, we have

\mathrm{Inf}(M\circ N)(n)=\bigoplus_{s\geq 0}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}+\dots+i_{k}=n\\

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope
}

To compare these two expressions, we use the maps

\lxSVG@closescope
}

}

\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
}

\lxSVG@closescope
